the rapporteur. - madam president, i also welcome our commissioner, the speed of parliament, the responsibility of the european parliament, is contrasted with the slowness and, let us say, a certain irresponsibility on the part of the council. we have already seen this when, on 16 september, we adopted the decision on own resources, which is the decision allowing the european commission to go to the markets to get 750 billion euros, and the council did not decide until later, on 14 december, and even used the fact that unanimity is necessary for some member states to blackmail others - i am reminded of the rule of law case, the rule of law case. now, at this moment, we are going to vote on the rules for implementing the decision which will allow the own resources decision to be implemented. we are once again in advance because the council, or rather the member states which must ratify this decision according to the constitutional rules of each member state, more than half of them have not yet ratified this decision. they're acting against themselves because it's an urgent need for us to have a response in terms of investment, in terms of health support for those who need it most because of the covid-19 pandemic. so i say: we have once again shown proactivity, we have given our consent and we have expressed our opinion that the council should decide immediately when this ratification is resolved. at the moment we are talking about the decision where there is only one new own resource, which is plastic, plastic packaging, but we have achieved something which is extremely important for the future: a binding timetable for the introduction of new own resources, so that we do not have cuts in future budgets, so that we do not burden citizens. it was important, commissioner, that this interinstitutional agreement was made known to all the ministers, because, although it was presented and approved by all the institutions, there are finance ministers and even prime ministers who are unaware that this agreement is binding and introduces new own resources with one objective: that of being enough for us to pay the debt resulting from the 750 billion euro for the next generation eu. because right now, from 2021 to 2027, the weight in the budget is only 0.01%, just over 12 billion euros, but after 2027 it will be 10% of the budget, this weight and this cost of debt. i would remind you that 390 billion of the 750 billion which goes into the budget of the european union and comes out as subsidies to the member states and the payment of this debt is assumed by the next budgets until 2058 and we cannot accept cuts in policies after 2027 and in funds, equivalent to 10% of the budget of the european union, to about 15 billion euros per year. on the other hand, we cannot burden the citizens, and so our principles are simple. those who benefit from the internal market and do not pay, must pay. so we want a tax on digital. in addition, it is right that there should be a carbon-adjustment mechanism at the borders which invites the exporting countries, the third countries, to respect the environment too. we shall have a revenue stream and, at the same time, fairer competition. the tax on financial transactions is also a necessity and then also the harmonization that means tax justice. commission accounts: each year we lose over €1 billion in fraud, tax evasion and avoidance, the equivalent of 7 annual budgets. this is truly unacceptable and it is demonstrated here that we can have more revenue without penalising the citizen and then there is another point that is necessary. we have always advocated new own resources, but they are now more urgent. we must avoid this idea of the juste retour, of i want my money back, this idea that budgets are bad and then there are net taxpayers and net beneficiaries. as i have said, everyone benefits from the european union budget. the own resources we defend protect the future, we must ensure the present, but we must also protect future generations, and our budget for the european union, which is just over 1% of gni, is nonetheless essential for our competitiveness, for the health union, for the digital union, so that we can respect the environment and, at the same time, have a union which is internally solidarity and cohesion and also be able to express and implement our external solidarity.
member of the commission. – mr president, last year was a remarkable year for all of us, but also for the european union budget. thanks to the multiannual financial framework (mff) and next generation eu (ngeu), the union is providing an unprecedented ambitious answer to an unprecedented crisis which has hit our economies and societies hard. it’s a demonstration of solidarity and a swift response at times of crisis. it will be the largest package ever financed through the union budget, at a total of eur 1.8 trillion. momentous decisions were also take on the financing side with the simplification of the vat-based own resource and the introduction of the plastic own resource. before that, the union own-resources system had remained stable over a long time, and the main categories of own resources have remained the same since the 1980s. traditional own-resources contributions from the member states are based on value added tax and on gross national income. i wish to thank the european parliament for its unwavering support to both maintain the vat-based own resource as a historic pillar of the existing system and to reinforce this system with the introduction of additional own resources. while the main elements of the vat own resource are maintained, the complexity of calculations and, therefore, the administrative burden for member states and the commission are significantly reduced. let me come now to the most notable novelty entering into force in 2021: the new own resource based on non-recycled plastic packaging waste. it is introducing a novel mechanism to encourage member states to achieve their environmental commitments without prescribing how. clearly, the ultimate success in reducing the quantity of plastic packaging waste that goes unrecycled depends on the actual increase of virtuous behaviours, using less plastic packaging while sorting and recycling more. to ensure such success, we must measure it credibly. the implementing regulations and the making available regulation provide the commission with adequate control powers and ensure that the commission is assisted by an expert group. these regulations also rely on sectoral legislation, which has recently been sharpened to improve the quality of reporting and the comparability of statistical data. this is crucial to guarantee equal treatment of all contributors. there remains one element in the package you are examining today which the commission cannot accept. the rapid review procedure that has been included in the making available regulation 2 (mar2) and the vat regulation by the council would put at risk the timely and comprehensive making available of resources by member states. therefore, we really welcome the support of the european parliament with regard to dropping this. in order for the own-resource system to function properly, we need a solid framework in place to ensure that we collect eu own resources effectively, in a timely manner and with minimum administrative burden. so far, the system in place has fulfilled the first two criteria as acknowledged in the annual discharge, where revenue has been a green area, and in eu ratings. the simplifications introduced in the own resources decision approved in december will contribute to a reduction of the administrative burden. to bring those changes into practice, it’s essential to agree on the implementing regulation as soon as possible. a delay would hamper the operationalisation of the new own resources decision. so far, 13 member states have ratified the decision, and we are confident that the process will be concluded in may, so that it can enter into force by june for the start of the ngeu borrowing.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, i should like to welcome commissioner hahn back to the european parliament. the own resources of the european union are a very important topic. the own resources allow us future stable and predictable financing of the european union so that in the future we can avoid politically divisive negotiations like the four-day, four-night council meeting of july 2020. the own resources of the european union also allow us to establish a comprehensive budget in the future: a robust budget, which allows us to properly finance all the priorities that the citizens of the european union expect us to deliver. the own resources are an important topic but they are also an urgent topic, because the whole eur 750 billion post-corona package to help people, to help enterprises, to help regions affected by the virus, depends on the quick ratification of the own resources decision by all national parliaments. here in the european parliament we have done our job. we have given our opinion on own resources quickly. we have negotiated the recovery and resilience facility and the multiannual financial framework quickly and we have set up our internal structures here in parliament for the implementation of the recovery and resilience facility. this is why it is of the utmost importance that all national parliaments ratify, by the end of april, the own resources decision so that help for people affected by the virus is coming. finally, i would like to ask all pro-european groups to make sure that their national parliaments, their parties in the national parliaments, do their utmost to ratify the own resources decision quickly.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, we’ve been waiting for the revision of the own resources system since 1988. and after more than 30 years, we are close to a historic turning point by approving tomorrow the updating of the implementing legislation regarding new own resources. finally, the european budget will become more autonomous, more transparent and stronger. we have a precise roadmap for the introduction of new own resources which won’t touch the pockets of our citizens but which will hit polluting companies, web-multinationals and fiscal free-riders more generally. i’m talking about the digital tax and the carbon border levy, to name but a few. but today it is less about the budget revenue side, and more about what it means to belong to a fiscally stronger european union; it is less about technicalities and more about political will and political challenges. to have a sound and responsible budgetary policy means, in the first place, to have the possibility to pay back the costs of the next generation eu that needs to be able to support the giant mobilisation of resources which will help citizens and companies to stand up again after a dramatic outbreak. it means to give hope and faith to the young generations who are completely lost in the middle of this never-ending pandemic, and it means to care for the most vulnerable people who need protection and solid welfare services, not isolation. our pride is based on the spirit and the sense of a community, the european community. and communities do work if we have a fair and good balance between the promises we make and the financial resources we collect in order to have these promises fulfilled. it is time to accomplish, to get things done – no time is left.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the containment measures adopted so far in the european union to contain covid-19 have demonstrated a clear and precise fact: the reduction in co2 emissions was 7 percent below the forecast, thus contradicting the mainstream environmental narrative. the sharp reduction in vehicle traffic and economic activity is therefore not matched by a corresponding reduction in air pollution; it is not sensible to block entire sectors of production and the free movement of goods in the name of spasmodic defence of the air and the environment. we must be more realistic in our analyses and proposals and undertake a thorough review of european policy, taking account of the geographical and climatic characteristics of each territory and focusing on the main anthropogenic sources of air pollution. it is very important to protect the environment, so: but let's never forget the economy and our states in crisis.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i congratulate mr ferrandino on the excellent work he has done, supported by the delegation. there are two key points to be made now. the first fundamental one is the call to member states to ensure that there is a really substantial quota for by-catches in traditional or artisanal fisheries, often cut out of quotas for administrative reasons or lack of representation in decision-making tables, but historically linked to this fishery. the second is the equitable distribution between the segments which already have considerable quotas, namely the small-scale fisheries, which often represent entire coastal communities; i think of porticello in santa flavia, sicily, for example. we follow article 17 of the basic regulation of the common fisheries policy, which calls on the member states to take account of craftsmanship and tradition when allocating national quotas for the various expenditure. the rapporteur has mentioned article 12 on the fair distribution of resources, which could help historic tuna such as the favignana to return. i therefore call on the european commission to make itself a genuine guarantor of the principles it proposes for a fair common fisheries policy, so as not to penalize and leave behind fishermen and shipping companies who must be protected even during the trilogue phase.

in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, as part of the economic recovery plan, the european globalisation adjustment fund, which we are renaming today, is an emergency instrument to help people who lose their jobs as a result of the global economic crisis. the lowering of the minimum threshold to 200 redundancies and the broadening of the scope are a first response to the many questions we asked before covid when, alongside the agenda of the new political priorities, we wanted to see the tools to compensate for the inevitable impact of the same on companies and workers. however, european contributions will now also be required for redundancies due to new reasons, such as changes in the composition of the internal market, automation, digitalisation, the transition to a low-carbon economy and also the consequences of the covid-19 pandemic. the european fund is therefore an instrument with which the european union is finally trying to compensate, at least partially, for the economic and social destabilization that, despite itself, will be caused by the agenda of new political priorities, to which the covid emergency, initially underestimated, has been added. we approve, commissioner, but we are not happy about it because it is still a tool to be used in extremely negative situations. our delegation has always supported the measures of this instrument, considering them as a partial return to the citizens and workers. we are therefore in solidarity with them.

president-in-office of the council. – madam president, thank you very much and my thanks to the commissioner too. honourable members, thank you for inviting the presidency to this joint debate and for all your efforts to adopt the european parliament mandate on the digital green certificate in such a short time. we look forward to engaging in negotiations as soon as possible with a view to reaching an agreement in the coming weeks so that the system can be put in place before the summer. this would give a strong signal to our citizens of our joint commitment to have europe on the move again. the council has welcomed the proposals on the digital green certificate put forward by the commission, which aims at facilitating freedom of movement within the eu. we also underline the flexibility that the proposed framework guarantees, and the presidency certainly hopes that the deployment of the digital green certificate will give a positive boost to the social and economic recovery of our societies. at the same time we have to be cautious and manage citizens’ expectations in light of the fast-changing evolution of the epidemiological situation in europe and across the world, in particular as regards tackling variants of concern, as we can unfortunately see at this moment. the council agreement on the negotiating mandate was unanimously supported by the member states, and our proposal already addresses some of the concerns of the parliament, including a sunset clause for the regulation. we also remain open to further discussions on fundamental issues of protection of personal data, taking into account the joint opinion of the european data protection board and the european data protection supervisor. in addition, at the end of last week, member states reached an agreement on guidelines describing the main technical specifications for the implementation of the digital green certificate. this is a crucial step for the establishment of the necessary infrastructure, both in the eu and at national level, and i would like to thank the commission for all the work done at this level. honourable members, as you know the question of accessibility and affordability of the covid-19 tests is not covered by the council mandate, nor is any question related to the fees. furthermore, the commission did not intend to regulate this issue on its proposal. however, the council and the member states are quite mindful of the importance of eliminating or reducing the costs incurred by their citizens and facilitating testing within the eu competence as provided by the treaty. allow me a couple of points on this matter. first, the council agreed in this mandate that the certificates themselves should be issued free of charge. second, following a council amendment in december 2020 to the directive on the common system of value added tax, member states will be able to apply either reduced or zero rates to testing kits, which was previously not possible. third, regarding testing, costs can also be reduced through joint procurement or eu funding. member states are receiving over 20 million rapid antigen tests through the emergency support instrument, while a joint procurement with 32 countries for over half a billion rapid tests was launched in november 2020. finally, although testing strategies are the competence of member states, in january the council adopted a recommendation setting out a common framework for the use of rapid antigen tests and the mutual recognition of covid-19 test results across the eu. furthermore, the health security committee agreed to a common list of covid-19 rapid antigen tests and a common standardised set of data for test result certificates. i’m sure these efforts will continue during the forthcoming negotiations with the european parliament because we have the same aim – to facilitate the lives of our citizens at this very difficult time so that families can be reunited and so that students and tourists and business people can travel, finally regaining a sense of normality and putting our economy on the move.
member of the commission. madam president, i would like to thank ladies and gentlemen, after more than a year of the covid-19 pandemic, we must remain vigilant, but we also have reasons for hope. the european vaccination campaign is accelerating. the last few months have been marked by numerous restrictions, including lockdown measures put in place to protect lives. today we want to give european citizens a hope. the commission is proposing a tool that will facilitate free movement throughout the european union, a tool that will be issued and accepted in all member states, thus allowing european citizens to travel safely and without discrimination throughout the union, a tool that will support the recovery of economic activities and could serve as a model for future solutions developed at international level. this tool, ladies and gentlemen, is the digital green certificate. the digital green certificate aims to facilitate free movement, at a time when we still need to live with the virus when some of us have already been vaccinated, while others have not because they are not yet priority level or because they don't want to be vaccinated. the certificate concerns the intra-eu cross-border travel of eu citizens and their family members, together with a twin proposal extending the system to third country nationals legally staying or residing in an eu member state and moving to another member state. of course, when developing the technical solutions, we will seek to ensure coherence with international standards that are currently being developed. the proposal is firmly built on two core values: non-discrimination and data protection. the digital green certificate will be available to all europeans, regardless of whether they are vaccinated or not. and it will ensure a high level of data protection, with a decentralized system of verification, using public key infrastructure. i welcome the support of parliament to make sure that the sensitive data included in the certificates is properly protected. this is crucial to ensure citizens' trust in the system. we need swift action to have a reliable and secure tool in place as soon as possible. time is of the essence, and we need to ensure roll out before the summer. technical work is progressing well to implement a solution that avoids centralization of personal data, minimizes the use of personal data and ensures security. i call on your support for this approach. the commission stands ready to address parliament's concerns on data protection and other points during the upcoming trilogue negotiations. if we can deliver politically, the technical solution will be ready in time. if we don't, we risk fragmentation across europe, with a multitude of possibly incompatible national solutions. we would risk having a variety of documents that cannot be read and verified in other member states. and we risk the spread of forged documents and, with this, the spread of both the virus and the mistrust of citizens. while putting in place the digital green certificate, we have to live with the fact that there are still scientific uncertainties regarding covid-19. we do not yet have full scientific evidence about the effects of vaccination or recovery from the virus. but we can't wait for all these questions to be answered to start taking action. we have to act now, by adopting a text that is sufficiently flexible to take these developments into account once more scientific knowledge becomes available. for this reason, the commission proposes to keep separate, for the time being, the digital green certificate the tool that we aim to establish with this regulation and its possible uses. we should not get stuck on arguments about whether or not to lift restrictions for the holders of such certificates. we will not be able to solve them at this point in time. in particular, new variants of concern are raising significant uncertainty. the difficult discussion on the use of the certificate has the potential to prevent us from moving forward in a pragmatic way. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the use of certificates. if member states wish to use the certificates for other purposes, they would need a legal basis at national level, in national law, which will have to be fully compliant with the general data protection regulation, and would need to prove that such use is necessary and proportionate, in consultation with their national data protection authorities. the commission would advise against prohibiting the use of the digital green certificate on such a national legal basis. otherwise, the member states that want to use the certificates domestically would be likely to create a separate national system. the result would be a worrying fragmentation in the european union. let me now make a couple of points on the accessibility and affordability of covid-19 tests. the digital green certificate will be free of charge, and we share the view that testing should be affordable for all citizens. but issues related to the reimbursement of medical costs, such as those for covid-19 tests, fall within the competence of the member states. this is a complex field, with many different public and private actors involved. more fundamentally, the impact on the availability of tests would have to be carefully considered, given that we are still fighting the pandemic. for its part, the commission has mobilised eur 100 million to purchase over 20 million rapid antigen tests, which have been delivered to the member states. the option to purchase an additional 400 million tests has been secured through our joint procurement mechanism. member states have placed orders for 40 million tests, and are of course encouraged to purchase more if there is a need. such joint procedures allow us to secure better conditions, including prices, for individual member states. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, it is obvious that we will not be back to normal this summer. to contain the virus, some measures will have to be maintained, but as of the coming summer and to help citizens travel safely in europe again, we need reliable and interoperable documents: we need the digital green certificate. i therefore urge you to act swiftly in the interests of european citizens and the internal market. the commission is ready to work tirelessly to support the search for a compromise acceptable to the co-legislators, and to do so as quickly as possible. i thank you for your attention and i am of course glad to hear your questions and comments, which we will certainly try to answer during the trilogue.
the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner reynders, mrs zacarias, for a long time now, the health emergency declared by the world health organisation has been shaking the globe and, consequently, the european union its 27 member states and its 450 million citizens with unprecedented virulence. clearly, the changes we have seen have certainly not been for the better. no, they have been put to the test of the constitutional structures of the member states and of the law of the european union itself. many member states have adopted emergency measures whose coherence with their constitutional architectures has been highly controversial, but in the european union we have seen an unprecedented onslaught on the most precious asset of european integration, which is schengen. schengen is the free movement of persons, a fundamental right of european citizenship, the most cherished by far in the european experience. we have said here in this european parliament that as a result of emergency measures unilaterally imposed by the member states, often in contradiction with european law - with the schengen code, which is a law, which is a regulation binding on the member states - schengen has been at risk as never before. we have repeatedly urged the commission to take the initiative to ensure an orderly restoration of free movement, convinced as we are that without free movement there is no european union. furthermore, the argument is reinforced, because, without free circulation, there will be no economic or social recovery after the devastation caused by the covid-19 pandemic, which has hit those economies very hard, which are very dependent on connectivity, which rests on the free circulation of people, but not only those in which tourism has a superlative weight in its gross domestic product, but to the set of open economies. that is why the commission has taken this initiative to relaunch schengen and free movement, and commission president ursula von der leyen is calling for the urgency procedure. the alleged legal basis is free movement, a fundamental right. the committee responsible is therefore the one i have the honour of chairing, the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, and i am also grateful for the honour of being the rapporteur, accompanied by an excellent negotiating team with the spokesmen of each of the parliamentary groups. we have worked quickly because we have supported the urgency procedure, and we have supported not only the urgency procedure but also the objective: to try to help, to help restore free movement. but it will not surprise anyone that the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs has placed emphasis on fundamental rights, on the principles of necessity and proportionality which govern the charter of fundamental rights of the european union and its application in the member states. this means that the data entered in the certificate must be the minimum necessary to produce its effect, which is the certification of the authenticity, integrity and validity of the data identifying the holder for the purpose of free movement at the internal borders of the member states, which is good news for cross-border workers, for temporary workers, but also for transport operators and for those who want to revive tourism in the european union, as we discussed yesterday in the european parliament. we also demand other conditions which are consistent with our concern for the fundamental rights of confidentiality and privacy, enshrined as fundamental rights in the charter of fundamental rights of the european union. this means that the data cannot be stored by any european central database, but only by the health authority issuing the certificate, which must be automatically delivered to the holder in the case of vaccines and in the case of pcr tests. furthermore, such data may not be used for any other purpose limitation of the purpose, which is to re-guarantee free movement and therefore may not be used internally by the member states either to go to a restaurant, to go to a theatre or to go to public spaces. the effect is to encourage the free movement of persons. we also ensure that there is adequate accessibility for people with disabilities, consistent with the directive we adopted in 2019 and that allows in a homogeneous way that those certificates, whether in paper or digital form, are also accessible to people with disabilities. we are concerned that the instrument is temporarily limited to one year and that the commission should regularly, periodically, three months before the expiry of the year of validity, inform the european parliament of its performance, because we are aware that there is an epidemiological dimension to which this parliament cannot respond, because it is the responsibility of science and pharmacology, where appropriate. but this parliament can establish the legal conditions under which we can assess the performance of this instrument over its one-year period of operation. we also want the whole legal framework for this certification to put an end to the chaotic situation we are in, the accumulation of contradictory and discriminatory measures, so that the effect is a uniform, uniform certificate valid in all the member states, and thus an end to this cacophony of measures which hold back so many people from taking the initiative, from returning to free movement, for fear of being exposed to additional restrictive measures in addition to the certificate. i would therefore like to stress two more points and conclude, madam president: firstly, the certificate must be free of charge and so must the tests, because tests are prohibited in some member states and a test which is compulsory cannot be priced prohibitively and discriminatory; and secondly, the states must not impose additional restrictive measures, the certificate must be sufficient to stimulate free movement. we have run the half-distance to the commission and to the council. we can only hope that, in the interinstitutional negotiation and tripartite dialogue phase, the council will go the middle way towards the european parliament and that we will be able to reach an understanding or an agreement so that the certificate will come into force in june, as the commission has proposed under the urgency procedure.

with the vaccination campaign in europe gaining pace, it is also now important to show perspective. today we show just how important this is for us by adopting this proposal – our proposal – in this urgent procedure without compromising on quality because – and i’m sure the commissioner doesn’t mind me saying – we make his proposal even better. firstly, because now the certificate will actually mean something. it will not just be another app or a piece of paper adding bureaucracy, but it will really help citizens enjoy their right of free movement and it will limit restrictions. it will make life easier. secondly, the certificate can be trusted. only vaccinations approved by the european medicines agency (ema) or the who will be covered, so that all member states and all citizens can trust in the reliability and the safety of the vaccines. we really emphasise this important role of ema, and of course all vaccine producers are invited to submit to this procedure. thirdly, this certificate now ensures full equality and it avoids discrimination between those who have been vaccinated and those who have not, or will not, be vaccinated by also including a test or a recovery certificate and by making sure that testing in connection with the certificate is always free of charge. lastly, words matter. the certificate was never green, it was only partly digital, and i haven’t met a single eu citizen that understood why it was called a digital green certificate. ‘eu covid—19 certificate’ is clearer, it’s better, and it also underlines one important aspect, namely and it would be good to find a common solution, because the certificates could become the starting point for a genuine european solution, beyond the freedom of travel for vaccinated, tested or recovered persons, which would still be centred on vaccinating all those who want to be vaccinated. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. make a real contribution to the fight against the pandemic and to freedom of movement.

in the name of the id group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would first of all like to clear the air of any doubt about this certificate, starting with the fact that it does not require vaccination, it is not a passport, it is not obligatory to use it, it does not restrict anyone's movements, on the contrary, it facilitates them. it is a tool which will serve to harmonize the rules of travel in the union in order to make it possible to revive tourism without the uncertainties experienced today by travellers, mainly due to the current situation at the borders, with rules that change every week and from one state to another and which have created inconvenience for passengers who have, for example, been denied boarding for an untranslated stamp. it's just a certificate for those who want to travel that can prove alternatively that they've tested negative for covid, that they're cured or that they've been vaccinated. today, too, a negative stamp is required for travel. nothing new or unusual. the covid certificate introduces additional possibilities, which are denied today by the measures in force for those who have recovered and for those who have decided to be vaccinated. the document we are voting on in the run-up to the negotiations with the council is an improvement on the commission's proposal. the work we have done in the committee in recent weeks with concrete proposals to protect citizens has led us to strengthen the protection of personal data, to call on the member states to make stamps free and available everywhere, starting from airports and stations, to avoid discrimination linked to the cost of stamps or the area of residence, and to insert a certain expiry date for the validity of the certificate itself. in addition to the health needs, we cannot forget a sector, that of transport and tourism, which represents 10% of european gdp and provides jobs and income for many families, especially in southern europe. therefore, i believe that in this phase of uncertainty linked to the pandemic, it is essential to abandon ideological positions and work for concrete solutions, respecting the rights and choices of everyone, to standardize travel rules in preparation for the summer season, also avoiding bilateral agreements between states, and to accelerate economic recovery. the covid certificate, while not perfect, though superfluous for some, only goes in this direction.

in writing. after more than a year of restrictions and limitations on travel in europe, the covid certificate represents a chance for european citizens to travel within europe again. it is not a vaccine passport, it does not impose any vaccination obligation, but it is a possibility to move between countries again. the certificate will not be required to enter a bar, a gym or a cinema, but only to move between eu countries. we are in fact strongly opposed to any form of compulsory vaccination or passport: everyone's freedom comes first. as an alternative to the vaccine, the person will be able to demonstrate tampon-negative or recovery from covid. to this end, we have insisted on free samples, in a way that does not create discrimination. at the same time, we must give hope to the tourism sector, which in italy represents a good share of the national gdp and has suffered too much in this year and a half of the pandemic: the resumption of tourism this summer can truly represent the resumption of the whole of italy, ending a season of closures and sacrifices.

in the name of the id group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are voting today on the just transition fund, a high-profile measure which will actually finance projects and works which could have been financed through the regional development fund anyway. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the answer to the question of who is to gain from this is that the country allocation table is a disgrace to many of us, as it shows that two countries, germany and poland, will receive almost a third of the total available funds. and if romania and the czech republic are added to these, four countries will receive more than half of the total allocation. doesn't seem like fair treatment for the other 23. but above all, despite the stated aim of providing support for the energy transition without leaving anyone behind, it was decided to exclude from the fund companies in difficulty, such as ilva of taranto, the main steelworks in europe, which could have benefited from european funding to convert production to green standards, protecting the jobs of its over 8 000 employees. in addition, any possibility of financing investments in natural gas, although initially recognised as eligible as a tool and bridge infrastructure to reduce emissions and meet the deadlines set in the transition pathway, has been totally excluded. in short, those who have been most diligent in switching back to renewable sources will get a few pennies, while those who have kept the coal mines open to date will get billions of euros. there are many reasons why we are not satisfied with the way this fund has been defined. but we are a serious and responsible government and, in order not to deny our companies financial support which may also reach italy in small part, we will not oppose it.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i was the shadow rapporteur for the id group on this matter for the agri committee. we believe that the just transition fund is a tool which, if used intelligently, can bring serious benefits to europe. but we note that the distribution of funding from the just transition fund is unfair, because it favours countries which have not yet taken the climate change issue seriously and have continued to produce coal and carbon, to the detriment of the more virtuous countries. we therefore believe that it will penalize those who, like italy, have made efforts to achieve the energy mix, meeting the objectives and who, unfortunately, will only have a marginal part of these sums. the introduction of the green reward mechanism also disadvantages italy and rewards those who have been negligent over the years. a less idealistic and more pragmatic approach to the environment is needed, as all the alternatives to achieving the objectives gradually and progressively need to be considered.
in writing. the just transition fund, designed to help eu countries cope with the social and economic impact of the transition to a zero-emission economy and the switch to cleaner technologies, should certainly be supported. in this process, however, the italian government has identified two areas, taranto and sulcis-iglesiente, which are among the most affected by the economic and social effects of the transition due to job losses linked to the production of fossil fuels and the transformation of industrial plants with higher greenhouse gas intensity. there is no doubt about the importance, at european rather than national level, of the steel industry located in those areas. however, the previous italian government did not take into account the willingness of the eu commission (see reply of 26.3.2020 to question p-001211/2020) to assess the inclusion of the industrial area of brindisi in the plan. it meets the same criteria and requirements as those laid down by the commission for participation in the investments of the just transition fund, namely the amount of co2 emitted by the industrial sector, the use of fossil fuels and the impact of the energy transition on land use. the brindisi area, however, deserves at least to be considered an eligible area, if only to be admitted to a subsequent financing phase.

member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members of parliament, thank you for this opportunity to discuss this important issue. the debate is very timely, as was said by the author himself, in view of the decision of the un general assembly to convene a high-level meeting on hiv and aids in june. as the world tackles the covid pandemic, the devastating aids global pandemic continues. while the world focuses on overcoming the direct impact of covid and laying the ground for recovery, the secondary health impacts, the disruptions to essential health services, including with respect to hiv, hepatitis, tuberculosis and malaria, are deeply concerning. we all know that hiv is fuelled by inequalities and that it disproportionately affects vulnerable and marginalised populations. worsening inequalities increase the spread of hiv as do armed conflicts and natural disasters. we need to work collectively to ensure that our response to the covid pandemic tackles these inequalities, preserve the gains achieved during the last decade and secures access for key populations to the essential services. this is why the commission welcomes the new global aids strategy for 2021-2026, sharing many of its priorities, not least the focus on tackling inequalities, gender equality and human rights. putting people at the centre, involving communities and ensuring that we learn from covid, in particular to build sustainable health systems is fundamental. this is why we welcome the broad principles, reiterated commitments and strong calls contained in the resolution tabled today. the european union is actively involved in the negotiation of an ambitious and progressive new political declaration for the upcoming high-level meeting on hiv-aids. as with previous united nations political declarations, the most sensitive issues will require delicate negotiations. on behalf of the european union, the commission is recommending the inclusion, not only of new prevention, testing and treatment targets, but also of specific targets related to stigma, discrimination and human rights. i am convinced that the fight against hiv is closely linked to breaking down barriers, including working on legal issues ensuring equitable access of key populations to services and treatments and strengthening the overall health systems, accompanied by an enabling environment for sustainable financial resources, notably domestic. the response to the hiv pandemic also requires further focus on adolescent girls and young women. our new gender action plan 3, puts the fight against gender inequality at the centre of our action. the commission is a long-standing supporter of the fight against hiv. we are a major contributor to the global fund with eur 550 million for the period 2020-2022 and we attach high importance to fighting hiv-aids globally. our more recent support to the work of the act accelerator, notably its cross-cutting health system connector, is paramount to mitigating the indirect effects of the covid pandemic and to strengthening the preparedness and response to current and future pandemics, and this includes hiv-aids. the upcoming high-level meeting provides an important opportunity to remobilise the international community with respect to the fight against hiv-aids, and you can count on the eu’s engagement.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, aids is a terrible disease, one that has been removed from our agendas, one that is often forgotten, but one that is very much a part of our daily lives. the picture, as mr tobé has already mentioned, is dramatic: in 2019, the number of people living with hiv was 38 million: of these 12 million, they had no access to life-saving retroviral therapy. the death toll was 700,000. sub-saharan africa continues to be the most affected region, with 57% of all new hiv infections and 84% of hiv infections in children. the next united nations general assembly must make a strong and renewed global commitment to eradicate this disease, as the commissioner has already said. we must ensure that care is provided which is too often blocked by the logic of profit. strong attention is also needed to guarantee the right to sexuality and to combat discrimination, the criminalisation of homosexual and transsexual persons, and gender inequalities: even today, in some parts of the world, we are facing a ghettoization that is conducive to disease.
madam president, commissioner gentiloni, ladies and gentlemen, i thank the commission for sharing the documents, as required by the recovery and resilience instrument regulation. however, i would like to raise a point that is close to my heart in terms of transparency: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the information is available to the public. these plans are essential for the citizens and we must not forget that they are the best instrument we have for scrutiny and control of public expenditure. we can assess, on the basis of the documentation provided, whether the objectives and constraints are being met, but it is above all our duty to ensure maximum transparency for the citizens, who are the real taxpayers and the real beneficiaries of this instrument. i therefore ask the commission to make an effort to do so: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft budget.
in writing. most member states have now submitted their national recovery plans, which are currently being assessed. a dialogue with the commission has begun, and the commissioners have confirmed that the commission will share its assessment of the plans with parliament. the process of trilogue, which was discussed between the parties and is enshrined in the rrf legislation, is well underway, and all participants are doing their job. but parliament is unhappy again, nothing is enough for it. we should remember that we lost months last year too because the ep tried to force the rule of law mechanism to create political conditions for the eu's financial sanctions. self-serving, pseudo-democratic actions like today's debate are not about speeding up vaccinations or restoring the economy. parliament has completely lost its role in the treaties and is seeking to enforce them. the commission assesses the national plans and the council adopts them. i ask my colleagues not to try to politicise it or to influence the commission's professional work. today's comments from the left have shown that they want to punish certain countries again; their goal is to deprive germans and poles of resources. i am proud that hungary has submitted a national recovery plan that would allocate 42% of the funds to green and 34% to health development. as a responsible budget advocate, we are not taking out loans for the time being, and we are actually launching programs at the expense of the national budget in order to get the hungarian economy to resources as soon as possible.
in writing. national recovery and resilience plans negotiated by member states with the european commission must offer a real and sustainable development prospect for european citizens. the resources made available must ensure the sustainable and digital transformation of european society through concrete mechanisms designed to reduce inequalities and develop opportunities for future generations. investment in education (for which we initiated the european parliament's request that at least 10% of the gni go to this area), improving access to quality healthcare and developing a resilient economic environment that respects the highest social standards are key priorities. the european parliament's right to information should be respected in order to increase the transparency of the development, evaluation and implementation of the national recovery plans. the current member state governments should not decide on the development of the plans themselves, as their implementation will take place until 2026, when some executives will end their mandates. access by the european parliament to the ongoing evaluation of the national plans is necessary to ensure a transparent procedure for the evaluation and implementation of the nrps. the commission should respect and support the role of the european parliament by providing appropriate access to materials and documents.
in writing. the parliament has concluded that the review process of the recovery and resilience mechanism does not meet the requirements. the commission has a duty to inform parliament about the process of evaluating member states' recovery and resilience plans. this information shall not be withheld and shall be provided in an understandable format. this should not be used as a means to irresponsibly allocate funds specifically earmarked for the recovery of each member state from the consequences of the covid-19 pandemic. these processes are in place to ensure full transparency and accountability and the democratic legitimacy of the plans. the plans should reflect the state's engagement with all relevant stakeholders, in particular local and regional authorities and civil society. i call on my colleagues to support this resolution and to engage in an open and constructive dialogue on recovery and resilience with the european commission.
the next item is the debate on the commission statement on the revised industrial strategy for europe (2021/2669(rsp)).
executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, it’s a pleasure for me to be here today to present the updates of our industrial strategy, and with that, also our proposal to tackle foreign subsidies. both initiatives are based on european strengths: openness to trade, fairness in competition and diversity in size: countries, as well as businesses, strong as a whole. just a year ago, we presented our industrial strategy to help europe’s industry lead the twin transitions and become more competitive globally. the strategy built on our strong european industrial base, the diversity and the talents of people, and our innovative capacity. we have received many valuable inputs from this very house, from the resolution on a new industrial strategy from rapporteur, mr calenda, and from many other of your reports on the green transition and the digital transition. our ambitions have not changed. we still need to fully implement the strategy of last year. we are updating it with what we learned during the pandemic. people in many businesses have suffered, and this goes for a number of sectors, in particular tourism, cultural and creative industries, textiles or mobility. at the same time, this year has shown our economies and our societies at their best. we have seen the healthcare professionals, the frontline workers, continuing to do their jobs despite very difficult circumstances. essential services have continued to deliver throughout the crisis, and this shows the fundamental resilience of our economy. many companies – and that of course also goes for small and medium—sized companies – have switched production to meet urgent needs, whether that be for personal protective equipment, hand sanitisers or, more recently, vaccines. many others have made the switch to e-commerce and used digital tools to find new suppliers. the past year has shown us how blockages to the free movement of persons, goods and services can harm our economies. disruptions in supply chains have challenged us heavily. we felt more strongly than ever that the single market is our most important asset, and we have to put all our efforts into making that work. we have also proposed a single market emergency instrument to help us react more urgently, more quickly, in the event of a future crisis, to offer immediate solutions to facilitate the circulation of persons, goods and services, and to improve transparency on intra—eu exports and service restrictions. some disruptions that we have seen through the last year were of a more global nature. indeed, they have at times put at risk access to products that we cannot do without. as it’s important to base our policies on facts, we have carried out a bottom-up mapping of more than 5 000 different products and identified 137 products where the eu can be considered highly dependent on imports from third countries. most of those dependencies can be addressed through, for example, diversification of supply chains. there are, however, 34 products which are potentially more vulnerable, given their possible low potential for further diversification and substitution with eu production. the majority of them belong to the energy-intensive industries and the health ecosystems. we need to go further to engage with member states and industry to deepen our analysis and to address the strategic dependencies where needed, while safeguarding the open, competitive and trade—based eu economy. we need to strike a careful balance between strengthening our own capacity in strategic areas and ensuring that the eu reinforces its position in the global value chains, for example by diversifying external trade and cooperating with our external partners. this is what open strategic autonomy is all about. a genuine dialogue with industry and other stakeholders is crucial for us. i’m so happy that we have already launched the industrial forum, because it will help us to shape future directions. we are building on the productive experience of the european battery alliance (eba) led by my colleague, maroš šefčovič. we are developing similar initiatives in the areas of raw material and clean hydrogen, and we are preparing the launch of alliances on processors and semiconductor technologies, as well as industrial data, edge and cloud. together with the update of the industrial strategy, we also tabled a landmark proposal for a regulation on distortive foreign subsidies. with this proposal, we are putting our industrial strategy into practice by creating the conditions for fair and competitive internal markets where all companies compete on their merits. we are responding to the call from this parliament to have a level playing field. europe is a trade and investment superpower. in 2019, the stock of foreign direct investment in europe was worth more than eur 7 trillion. that’s a quarter of all foreign direct investment in the world. and that openness to investment serves us well. but openness requires fairness. for more than 60 years, our state aid rules have made sure that aid from european governments doesn’t undermine fair competition in our markets. the companies have been free to use subsidies granted by third-country governments to buy up businesses here in europe. some have been able to undercut their competitors in public tenders, not because they are more efficient but because they get unfair financial support. under the proposed regulation, companies which get subsidies from non-eu countries will need our approval under this very framework before they implement big mergers in europe. companies will have to tell us what subsidies they have received when they bid in large public tenders, and they won’t be able to close that tender without our ok. we will have the power on our own initiative to investigate any company to see if it has received foreign subsidies that harm competition in europe. we’re making sure that we have the right powers to put these rules into everyday practice. europe’s economy is open and closely interlinked with the rest of the world, and we are committed to keep it that way. at the same time, we will act assertively when that is needed to keep our economy working well for its citizens. finally, we continue our review of competition rules to make sure that they also support the green and digital transition, of course while staying true to the fundamental principles of fair competition in a single open market to the benefit of all europeans. (applause)
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, the epp wants european industry to become sustainable in europe. it wants industry to adopt digitalisation in europe. it wants european industry to thrive in europe. any plan for europe’s green and digital transition will only work if european industry is an integral part of the plan. we welcome the commission’s updated strategy, but we still believe it’s a work in progress. we strongly support the idea of this ecosystem approach. these one-fits-all strategies of the past of industrial policy have been the reason why they have never been taken up neither by the industry nor by member states, and we do believe that the update presents a part of that analysis. it covers six out of the 14 sectors, but that shows it’s still a work in progress and most of these ecosystems are not completed in definition or in description; analysis alone is not enough, so what we want is for each ecosystem to have an integrated strategy setting priorities for the union in terms of funding policies and regulation and we do not want to have a planned economy, but we want that the instruments fit together. it needs to address the specific needs for competitiveness, sustainability and digitalisation of each ecosystem. it needs a reference frame to test the systems for each ecosystem. this ‘one-fits-all’ approach doesn’t work. taxonomy shows us that’s not a wrong instrument, but does it work for each sector? our industry needs this perspective now. the real implementation of the union strategy starts soon, in just one year. by that time 2030 is only eight-and-a-half years away. that is an extremely short time for an industrial transition. to say it loud and clear: at present we have 120 instruments, agenda-setting instruments, funding instruments, regulation instruments, trade instruments, and so far we need this final cornerstone of the strategy to reflect better and that works in the individual sectors. so in the end, if that doesn’t fit together we don’t have high hopes that climate change and the digitalisation of work is going to work.
i would like to thank mrs vestager, on behalf of the s&d group. we find the analytical part of the commission's communication very agreeable, the assessment of industrial dependence and the recognition of the need for stricter iprs, the need for one-in, one-out application to avoid over-regulation, ecosystems and the need to update competition policy very important. many of the strategic lines are reflected in our report on industrial policy, which was approved by the european parliament. however, there are some significant shortcomings which we cannot fail to note: the first is the concrete. this communication is too vague: the commission has not yet taken any action, and it is not yet ready to take any action. we are emerging from the worst economic crisis since the war, businesses are more indebted and more fragile, entire sectors are on their knees. i also see a great risk: the eu must proceed with setting ever stricter environmental standards without at the same time giving businesses the tools and resources to invest and defend themselves against the misconduct of our competitors. the strategy is not very effective in this respect. we have suggested the possibility of using european funds as a tax credit, we have addressed the question of gas as a transitional energy in our strategy and there is no trace of it! and, of course, ecosystems - i agree with my colleague - are an important step, but we have to define the toolkit, because european businesses are in a state of enormous difficulty and we have run out of time to intervene.

for ourselves, we’re also here for this planet when it comes to making sure that eventually everyone will be vaccinated. in europe half of the vaccines produced here are being exported. and that is what will gain us real ground for european industry to work because that is the key to opening the world and opening the markets that our industries depend on. what you will also see is that when it comes to the real driver of industry, which is the single market, we have a fully fledged not strategy but workings on the ground, hands-on every day. the first thing of course is the single market task force, because the problem is that there is a comeback in the single market. you saw it during the pandemic: the lack of mobility for people, for goods, for services; because we need common standards in services for this service market really to work. and here the single market task force is there with member states to make all of these barriers come down again, so within europe we can benefit from a single market. second, that we have a single market emergency tool because it will come back to haunt us that borders came up, that we had the queues of lorries, that it took some time for the commission actually to establish the green lanes. that will come back to haunt us, because that was not the case in the united states. that was not the case in many african countries. it was double that or triple that it would not be enough. we need to spend in order to crowd in private investment to really make this a real common effort for europe to be a competitive force, a sustainable competitive force on this planet, and this is of course what we are doing in building the different schemes, crowding in private investment. some have asked about the green side of it. well i hope everyone is looking forward to the fit for 55 package, building on the agreement in this very house. there is lots of inspiration coming from this house for the fit for 55 package, with which the industrial strategy should of course fit hand in glove. last but not least, i will in particular thank the many members who mentioned skills, because without skills we will have no industrial strategy. without digital experts, without the high level of digital skills, we will have no competitiveness. one of the main brakes on small and medium-sized businesses is the lack of qualified people to hire. this is a main effort for competitiveness that the basic skills are on a higher level, be that digital or otherwise, and that the specific skills really are top grade, and that of
