climate and the environment are concerned, i realise that they are the most divisive issues in this house. i am amazed that despite an unprecedented recession, no one, i mean no one, has considered whether very ambitious targets, the result of rules written before covid, are still feasible with these timelines, or whether on the contrary the roadmap needs to be revised. i spoke about it last year in connection with the single-use plastics directive, and i was accused of heresy. so let's have clean air, let's have clean water, we all want to do that, but without astronomical investments and above all without the sacrifice of those who work, these goals cannot be achieved.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, unfortunately we only think of smes when we need them. during the pandemic, in fact, with great effort and sacrifice smes have kept italy and europe standing. and now that we're coming out of the health emergency, they're still the ones who have to shoulder the burden of the economic recovery. i wonder where europe stood when it came to putting in place targeted policies to strengthen the sme system. plastic tax, green deal, nutriscore are all measures that are hurting and not supporting our entrepreneurial fabric. if we exclude the dossier brought to this house by the league, there is still no action plan for this strategic sector. in the meantime, smes are being burdened with new european charges, such as the plastic tax. they are asked to make rapid progress in digital innovation, but at the same time they are held back by an archaic and complex bureaucracy. they are expected to adapt to the green transition, but at the same time they are expected to remain competitive in markets dominated by commercial giants who are not polluting the environment. to these contradictions is added a new emergency: the raw materials. essential to manufacturing and the green and digital transition, they've become elusive and prohibitively expensive. europe has been tied to china for its supplies, unable to plan its own needs and unable to defend its economic interests. unfortunately, the smes, the cinderella of community policies, to whom, paradoxically, the salvation and future of europe is entrusted, will once again pay the price for these mistakes.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in my view the european strategy for small and mediumsized enterprises must be based on four main objectives: investment, including the creation of conditions for access to it to be a reality for all; innovation, as a facilitator of economic recovery and adaptation to the dual transition to green and digital; the internal market, including the digital internal market - we need a cohesive internal market without fragmentation; and, finally, the fight against red tape and the reduction of administrative costs for businesses. on this last point, since the epp group's ambition is to reduce these burdens on small and mediumsized enterprises by at least 30%, i ask the european commission: what will be done to make this simplification of procedures a reality in the legislative packages on artificial intelligence and on digital services and markets that are now under consideration?
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, small and mediumsized enterprises are the economic lungs of our countries. in france, there are more than three million of them, and they account for more than 80% of jobs. and yet, we are constantly harassed: untenable social charges, flimsy administration, violent and unfair taxation with the little ones and so complacent with the fat ones. the european union is pushing our nations into unbridled free trade and is subjecting our companies to unfair competition from multinational companies. after the disastrous consequences of the european union's health and economic choices, financial arrangements will unfortunately not be enough to save many of them from bankruptcy. and while the pandemic has accelerated a shift in society in favour of multinationals, leading to the systematic disappearance of small and medium-sized enterprises, it must not lead us into an irreversible process of globalisation and social change that we reject. localism, short circuits and national preference for public procurement are all solutions which must be urgently put in place to overcome the crisis.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, improving the way the european union legislates and ensuring that legislation better serves citizens and businesses is a necessary and indispensable process, especially in the difficult times we are in. for too long, there has been a perception of a considerable distance between legislators and citizens, making many laws appear to have been passed from above, without taking due account of the real needs of society and the production system. i therefore welcome the commissioner's reference to the implementation of the better regulation agenda. i hope, however, that this is not just talk, because the better regulation agenda will ensure the quality of regulation through a more flexible consultation system, a reduction of the burden on citizens and businesses that have suffered too much, compensating for new burdens by removing equivalent ones in the same sector, and finally a long-term approach rather than a short- and medium-term one.
in writing. microenterprises play a key role in the european economy and even more so in the economy of certain member states such as italy. this is why europe aims to be attractive for small business start-ups, growth and expansion, by supporting the transition to sustainability and digitalisation, reducing regulatory burdens and improving market access and financing. however, due to their peculiar characteristics, microenterprises have been the most affected by the 2020-2021 covid-19 crisis and the figures for the decline in turnover, as well as the abandonment or downsizing of planned investments are worrying: the eu commission itself states that it is unlikely that the value added of smes will return to 2019 levels by the end of 2021. a formal update of the "sme strategy for a sustainable and digital europe" presented in march 2020 that takes into account the impact of the covid-19 pandemic and the changed economic and international framework conditions no longer seems to be postponed. the commission should be able to assess the impact of the measures on the union industry in the light of the experience gained in the implementation of the measures.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european union's biodiversity strategy for 2030 is a plan to encourage the transition to more sustainable agriculture. the measures proposed by the commission must not become new burdens for our farmers. the objective of reserving at least 25% of agricultural land for organic farming by 2030 does not take account of the territorial realities and the characteristics of our farms. the choice of this type of production should be a free choice for our farmers and not a tax which would be particularly penalising for smaller farms. it is also essential to take account of the diversity of national and regional agricultural systems and their vulnerability to adverse climatic conditions, such as the recent severe damage to agriculture in the veneto and friuli-venezia giulia regions. the transition to more sustainable farming systems must not be an obligation but a gradual choice supported by adequate and practical economic support for our farmers. we do not accept the impositions of brussels. our agriculture must be helped and not penalized. biodiversity, yes, but with reasonable timing and methods.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the figures from the world tourism organisation are pitiless. in 2020, europe lost 70% of arrivals from abroad, putting 100-120 million jobs at risk, and tourism businesses saw a decline in turnover of over 37% between the summers of 2019 and 2020. we can't waste any more time. it is now time for citizens to decide where they will spend their holidays. we can't afford to lose another summer. and the european digital covid-19 certificate will become a reality as soon as possible. tourism and mobility in general are strategic sectors for the economies of our countries. this is a first step towards a gradual return to normalcy. the positive thing is that the negotiations have taken up what we have been saying for a long time: everyone will be free to choose whether to request it or not. we believe that freedom is a fundamental principle that must be defended at all times and in all circumstances. the return to normal also means that the certificate will be a temporary and extraordinary measure pending the restoration of conditions for full freedom of movement. the unvaccinated will be able to travel in europe as well as those who have been vaccinated, with a simple negative swab. we must therefore work to prevent any discrimination by ensuring that tampons are available at a reasonable cost. this is an important test bed for european policy. to the businesses and the tourism workers i say: we are here!
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the rrf system provides for the possibility of using 312.5 billion in subsidies for those member states which so request. however, it is well known that the commission has no own resources and the treaties expressly forbid expenditure not covered by revenue in advance, so that exceptionally it relies on the markets to raise them by issuing bonds which will have to be repaid both in capital and interest. i would like to express my doubts about the ability of the member states using these financial flows to raise funds exclusively through so-called 'own resources', i.e. by imposing new taxes to meet the commitments made by the commission in the form of bonds, especially since most of these countries have opted for the application of subsidies alone in their national recovery and resilience plans. if my forecasts prove correct, how will the commission meet its commitments? by further increasing the tax burden in the process?
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the analysis of the pnrrs submitted so far shows that only a few member states have planned to use the loan quota, and only for 166 billion. the most logical reason is that the implementing regulation contains cross-compliance which is considered to be disproportionate to the objectives. the commission has also proposed that the commission should adopt a new methodology for the calculation of the annual gni-based own resource for the period from 1 january 2019 to 31 december 2020 in order to ensure that the gni-based own resource is used in a manner that is consistent with the objectives of the union's macro-financial assistance. the choice of the "core" countries, such as germany, france, spain, belgium, the netherlands, austria and others, not to require the loan quota has as a further key to read the stringent usage constraints provided for in the next generation eu agenda. in fact, because of their investment type not tailored to the real needs of post-pandemic growth, they do not represent enough appeal to be considered cost-effective to use. this has led many countries to raise funds independently on the financial markets by issuing government bonds directly, without the above conditions, at rates higher by a few dozen basis points, which is also possible thanks to the ecb's action with the pepp programme. i would like to conclude by reminding you that it has been exactly 376 days since the next generation programme was announced, and yet not a penny has been seen, while throughout the rest of the world rivers of resources have already been poured into aid like never before in history.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the hijacking of a ryanair flight to allow the arrest of political opponent roman protasevich and his partner sofia sapega seems to be taken from a hollywood movie. unfortunately, this is yet another example of repression by an illiberal government such as lukashenko's. in this situation russia has lost a historic opportunity to distance itself from an increasingly unthinkable ally. the friendly meeting in sochi between putin and lukashenko makes moscow's position uncomfortable given the misdeeds of the belarusian dictator. but there's another actor in this scene, who's too little talked about: china. russia and china are missing from the list of nations that have not condemned the hijacking of the plane at the un security council. for beijing, belarus is a strategic corridor within the silk road, the political-commercial penetration route used as a key by the chinese communist party. and by the way, just a few days ago, china, through its ambassador in minsk, praised the belarusian government, not forgetting that, amidst the street protests, xi jinping was the first foreign president to congratulate lukashenko on winning the highly contested 2020 elections. belarus is china's gateway to central europe, and trade relations between the two countries are steadily increasing, while lithuania, which has understood what is behind the silk road, has left the contact group between china and central and eastern european countries. in addition to blocking flights from belarus, which is a bold and decisive move, the european union should block chinese train convoys coming from belarus. it would send a very strong signal to the dictator in minsk and his chinese supporters.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, when it comes to the un, people have a common opinion. the un is seen as a hyper-bureaucratic cart that burns $6 billion for institutional expenditure and almost $30 billion a year for the costs of the various agencies. is it supposed to be about settling disputes? she hasn't done that in years. in fact, she's more and more addicted to crossed veins. should it intervene where there is violence and war? it is no longer doing so, leaving many people to their fate. you talk about defending human rights, but the un is accepting as members an increasing number of non-democratic countries which commit flagrant violations of individual freedoms, and in particular religious freedoms. we have reached the paradox that in the un human rights council there are countries like china, cuba and pakistan. can you believe that? the un proposed the useless global compact on migration, but then it is totally absent in the management of reception. i mean, it's more and more like a propaganda organization. it is enough to think of the recent declaration by its secretary-general, who spoke of a 'global epidemic of islamophobia', when there are millions of christians throughout the world who are being killed and persecuted, but who are not being mentioned.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the agreement on the new european social fund plus brings together several instruments which i dealt with in the empl committee, including the programme to tackle the problem of youth unemployment, the programme to tackle child poverty and the european fund for the poor. under the agreement, eu countries where the number of children at high risk of poverty is higher than the eu average will have to spend at least 5% of the fund's resources on measures to help combat child poverty. in countries where the number of young people who are not currently working, studying or training is higher than the eu average, 12.5% of the fund will have to be spent on combating youth unemployment. parliament has negotiated to have a budget dedicated to the fight against extreme poverty in each member state. at least 3% of the resources will have to be spent on those who need it most, providing basic material assistance or addressing material deprivation. given that the new european social fund plus will thus have a financial envelope of eur 88 billion, 100 billion in current prices, we hope that national governments will now be able to identify correctly the beneficiaries of the fund and present the spending priorities in the national operational plans. the task of the salvini league will always be to stimulate the process. we are comforted by the fact that oftentimes history outside parliament gives us back the reason that is denied us in this house. we know how to govern, we show it every day and we are always among the people, and the people want the funds to be used in the right way.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we have returned to the plenary in strasbourg and the indications from our citizens are clear. people want to start again, young people want answers and children deserve a better future. almost one in five young people around the world have stopped working since the start of the pandemic. within the borders of the european union we have 15 million people at risk of poverty, including 5 million children. then welcome an instrument like the new european social fund plus which will make it possible to allocate some eur 90 billion to the member states. money to be spent on creating new job opportunities to break the cycle of poverty and invest in both the early years and the training of our young people for the world of work. money that, as we have strongly requested, can be earmarked to deal with exceptional circumstances such as the pandemic. we would have liked more flexibility for the member states, but overall the proposal we have come up with is a sensible one. if we really want to get things started again, we cannot forget the most vulnerable people: the children, the young and the poor of europe.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, adaptation and mitigation: these are the concepts that should guide future measures to counter the effects of climate change. making european fisheries and aquaculture more resilient requires a paradigm shift, from looking at the fisheries sector as the sole culprit for the depletion and pollution of the seas to understanding that fishermen and aquaculturists are among the first victims and should be involved in proposing solutions for the basin. the increase in water temperature and other environmental factors are contributing to phenomena that alter the balance of fish stocks. the evidence is the many jellyfish, the mucilage, and the invasion of the blue crab in the adriatic sea. it is a chain effect that has repercussions on native species, aquaculture and fisheries, processing, trade, community livelihoods, food security. an institutional response that addresses these needs in a flexible and rapid manner is needed. this is the only way to increase the environmental, economic and social resilience of all stakeholders and reduce their vulnerability. the introduction of ever more restrictive measures on fishing, such as those which are worrying the fishermen of the mediterranean, is nothing but a waste of energy, a breeding ground for distrust of the institutions and a source of tension and protest. on 12 june, italian fishermen will stop fishing to say enough is enough to the continuing restrictions. i appeal to commissioner sinkevičius: the commission is therefore proposing that the european parliament should visit our ports, review the management plans as soon as possible and adapt them to socioeconomic needs before it is too late. the eu cannot damage a strategic sector such as fisheries any longer.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, 2050 is a key date for our future and for our children's future, because by 2050 we must be good enough to zero in on the damage of climate change and go back and fix the mistakes that have been made. fishing and aquaculture play a key role in ensuring that the new society lives in harmony with the sea and therefore with the planet, and is resilient and willing to work. our shipping companies have suffered the abandonment of young people because the sector has been neglected, making things worse from an economic and environmental point of view, as the culture of caring for the sea has been lost. pollution, illegal fishing and poorly managed shipping did the rest. on this oceans day, i ask you: what concrete action does the commission intend to take? when will you send a proposal to parliament? the year 2050 is not waiting, it is just around the corner, and we need legally binding rules, a holistic approach to the sea and the actors that exploit it, not just in terms of fisheries, and a concrete response to the depletion of fisheries resources and the consequent depopulation of local coastal communities. saving and rejuvenating the marine environment is the highest form of protection and resilience possible. so i ask the commission not to make a mistake because we cannot afford it any longer and we will not get a second chance.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, while we are discussing the effects of climate change on fishermen, those who have to feed their families in the real world must be looking at this with puzzled eyes. if these upheavals are a threat to our fishermen, then we cannot ignore the immediate and disastrous consequences of offshore wind farms, both for their commercial activity and for the balance of our oceans. this economic sector is systematically sacrificed on the altar of the interests of international trade, often under the cover of an ecological hoax. instead, attack the factory ships and other 'scrombus' that are plundering the fauna and flora of our seas for the benefit of unscrupulous multinationals. if adapting the fisheries sector to the demands of climate change means imposing more rules, more regulations, more directives, inspections and policing of all kinds, for us it means no. our small-scale fishermen are the guardians of our oceans, they contribute to our food sovereignty. they are honest and sincere men and women who are fully aware of the need for reform in their profession, which must be done with them and not against them.
(mike's speech begins) ... also to consider the enormous impact that climate change is having on our seas and oceans. sea temperatures are rising, sea levels are rising, water is becoming more acidic, storms are becoming more violent, and plant and animal species are dying off. this debate is about how the fishing industry is being hit by climate change and ocean degradation. that's the world upside down. the fisheries sector itself is a major contributor to climate change and biodiversity loss. industrial fishing, and in particular bottom fishing, emits more co2 annually than all commercial aircraft worldwide combined. so the question is not how to protect fisheries against climate change. the question is: how do we protect the climate from fishing? and when will we stop the destructive fishing activities and overfishing of our oceans? i also believe that european agricultural and fisheries subsidies should be abolished.
madam president, climate change is certainly one of the great challenges facing the fisheries and aquaculture sectors. rising water temperatures and ocean acidification have a direct impact on marine species. these disturbances have an impact on the management of union waters and resources and on the way of life of coastal communities. in this context, the common fisheries policy needs to evolve to support the fisheries and aquaculture sectors and promote measures to promote their adaptation. for example, the principle of relative stability, which has barely changed since its inception 40 years ago, does not take into account the impact of climate change on the distribution of species. if we add to this brexit and the landing obligation, it is very difficult to maintain the current key for the allocation of fishery resources for much longer. on the other hand, we must not forget that fishermen, shellfish farmers and the rest of the wider marine industry chain are not the cause of climate change but its victims. moreover, fisheries and aquaculture are strategic sectors and i would say our allies in the fight against climate change. indeed, fish is often referred to as the perfect protein. it's the animal protein with the lowest carbon footprint and the lowest environmental impact. as part of the fight against climate change we must encourage the consumption of seafood. madam president, i remember very well the first time i came here to the european parliament in brussels. it was a hot and sunny september day, just after the summer break. i came out of the metro and there were cars, buses, and people speaking all kinds of languages all around me. as i was walking towards the european parliament, it really looked like this ginormous beehive made out of concrete and glass. it was humming, it was full of life while, at the same time, many things actually seemed a little bit improvised and maybe even messy. i must be honest that probably this building taught me more about the european union than what i had previously learned in school in textbooks or infographics. because this parliament, with its many doors and corridors, all the different shapes and styles, the sheer massiveness of the place, the extensions and annexes that came with the time, even the grey carpet and the art pieces all around the house, really are a perfect metaphor of the european union because they show our diversity and, at the same time, show the imperfection and incompleteness of this project. but, just like this building, just like any other building, the european union needs, more than anything, a strong fundament – no single market, no freedom of movement, no erasmus without a common basis that we can all stand on. and this fundament is actually pretty clearly spelled out in the treaties in article 2: democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights. but we all know that this fundament is under heavy attack. the basis that unites us all, the precondition for the existence of the european union, is being dismantled as we speak. but, instead of defending it, the european commission has been watching, monitoring and writing reports over the past years, without, i must say, obviously fully grasping the seriousness of the situation we are in and without seeing the urgent need to stabilise our common fundament because that needs action. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the rule of law is a serious matter. it is the greatest achievement of european and western civilization. the end of a long and not easy journey. it cannot be used as a weapon of political battle. you do not want to reduce it to a tool for forcing people to vote for one party or another. today there is a majority of the centre-left that uses the rule of law against centre-right governments, which are found guilty of wanting to represent the values they believe in and to defend those for which they have been given a fully democratic mandate. tomorrow the majority in this house may change, but the wreckage of this unjust and violent attack on the self-determination of peoples will remain. the economic blackmail of the peoples of europe, even in the face of the consequences of the worst pandemic of the modern era, is a vile choice, which does not honour the founding fathers of this union, which does not honour our future together.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, today in this parliament we are talking about the rule of law. yesterday in italy, for the umpteenth time, a magistrate was subjected to precautionary measures because, according to the prosecution, he would have sold his office for enrichment and career by fixing the processes. in the face of hundreds of magistrates who carry out their role in silence and with dedication, a system of appointments has emerged in italy that is conditioned by representatives of political parties and by currents and cordas within the judiciary itself. there are important judicial offices where the head has been decided with this client system, where independent and free-thinking judges are put in a corner and are prevented from working. there are judgments that have been piloted thanks to this system, alleged lodges or business committees that would have affected the administration of the law, investigations omitted or archived on the basis of the assumption "favours for friends, law for others". i ask you colleagues: in this situation, can we speak of respect for the rule of law in italy?
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, mr secretary of state, mr commissioner, the ambitious objectives set at the porto summit are an important step in the development of a europe that is finally social, but they risk remaining statements of principle if we do not change the foundations of the union. the european semester mechanism needs to be revised because it does not take account of the social fabric of a country. the recent rejection by the european commission of the italian government's block on redundancies is a denial of the evidence of a pandemic and a failure to know the national realities. this incomprehensible assessment contradicts everything we have been discussing with the european commission for more than a year on the european measures to be taken to support employment of the most vulnerable people in the labour market. the european semester must therefore have real social and environmental objectives as well as economic ones, and the stability pact must be dissolved by budgetary constraints that are now outdated by history, otherwise the benefits of the next generation eu will be cancelled as soon as the safeguard clause is deactivated, thus giving us back an austerity europe that the european citizens are no longer willing to accept.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, albert einstein said: 'the only thing that is certain is that the world will be a better place if we are able to work together'. "computers are incredibly fast, accurate and stupid. men are incredibly slow, inaccurate and intelligent. and the combination of the two is an unstoppable force. " one thing is certain: computers rule our lives, and there are intelligent but criminal men who use stupid machines to threaten our security. news reports report cyber attacks that threaten to bring entire nations to their knees by hitting industrial logistics infrastructure. a recent study tells us that 2020 was a disastrous year for cybersecurity. every hour, there are between 5,000 and 10,000 cyberattacks. we must reflect on the importance of protecting our cyberspace, which is still largely dominated by non-european players. the european union's cybersecurity strategy needs to be based on a number of key elements, such as the development of advanced processing systems located within the union, internal management of sensitive data and analysis of data flows to the outside world from devices imported into the union. we will endeavour to improve the commission's proposals in this field, always having as a guiding principle the protection of the union's businesses and citizens, their freedom and their security.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, europe is deluding itself that it is solving the problem of gender inequality with the anachronistic principle of the pink quota or with sterile nominalisms such as engineer, lawyer, minister. the emerging countries around us, on the other hand, are racing towards the future and are facing international competition in a pragmatic way, by enabling the mass incorporation of female talent into the technical and scientific professions. in neighbouring central asia, half of the total are stem graduates, 75% in burma alone. but china is spending 200 billion dollars a year on scientific research, and soon the number of chinese women scientists will explode. in europe, on the other hand, we are stuck at 36%, a figure that drops dramatically to 16% in italy. if we are not to fall behind not only in terms of equality but also in terms of technological and scientific progress, and therefore in terms of the economic and geopolitical challenges, we must intervene where the educational paths begin, by removing gender stereotypes and by training women for the professions of the future from primary school onwards. community studies say that we can achieve equal pay by 2050, with a 10% increase in gdp per capita in the european union. but all this on one condition: the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of the european union.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, the 2015 russian federal law 129 on undesirable activities of foreign and international non-governmental organisations, and its later, even more oppressive forms, have led to an ever more violent crackdown on civil society in russia and now to the listing of three german ngos as undesirable organisations. international cooperation to any civil society actor is key. they report their government’s misdeeds and violent abuses to the world and, if they are cut off from the world, they themselves are vulnerable to violent abuse. oppressive regimes have become more open and brazen in their silencing of dissidents abroad. criminal states even hunt down dissidents abroad. the hijacking of a plane is a crime that can be prosecuted. so is limiting in any way the operation of the european civil society.
member of the commission. – mr president, the european union has a longstanding and clear position on russia’s foreign agent law. the legislation goes against human rights and commitments, which russia has assumed under european and international human rights law, including the european convention on human rights of the council of europe. russia’s list of undesirable organisations is growing, including european ngos. this leaves russian civil society activists and knowledgeable and engaged experts and partners more isolated. the decision taken by the russian prosecutor general on 26 may to list three german ngos as undesirable organisations is the most recent example of the russian authorities’ disregard for a vibrant civil society. this decision ultimately harms the interests of russian people and possibilities for open and free dialogue. the european union has repeatedly condemned this foreign agent legislation and called on the russian authorities to repeal it. it is essential that russian citizens are able to exercise freedom of association, including the right to join and participate in non—governmental organisations, which promote the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms. on 31 may, andrei pivovarov, a former executive director of open russia, was removed from a plane in st petersburg and detained for alleged violations of the law on so—called undesirable organisations. the eu has called on the russian authorities to immediately and unconditionally release him. this case is not an isolated incident, but confirms a continuous pattern of shrinking space for civil society, the opposition, critical voices and the independent media in russia. the european union fully supports the key role played by independent ngos in developing mutual understanding across borders. we will continue supporting russian human rights activists and civil society, as we do worldwide. we hear from those activists that our support is vital and greatly appreciated. we will keep monitoring developments in russia, holding the russian authorities to account when they do not respect the fundamental rights enshrined in its constitution and international conventions. we will insist on the need to respect the decisions of the strasbourg court and in general the council of europe conventions that anchor russia and the eu in democracy, the rule of law and respect for human rights.

the next item is the debate on the request for an oral answer to the commission on autism and inclusive employment by lucia ďuriš nicholsonová, rosa estaràs ferragut, pierfrancesco majorino, jordi cañas, stefania zambelli, katrin langensiepen and josé gusmão, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs (o-000017/2021 b9-0017/2021) (2020/2585 ((rsp)).
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, the european commission is committed to protecting and promoting the rights of persons with disabilities, including, of course, those on the autism spectrum. in doing so, we focus on areas where eu action can bring the highest added value, and we call on member states to take action in their areas of responsibility, in line with the un convention on the rights of persons with disabilities. in addition, the european pillar of social rights recognises the right of persons with disabilities to equal treatment, and opportunities, and to support ensuring a life, and dignity, and participation in the labour market and in society. the strategy for the rights of persons with disabilities, adopted by the commission in march, takes into account the diversity of disability, which is sometimes invisible. this is often the case for people on the autism spectrum. for them, full inclusion in society is not obvious. challenges are perpetrated by persistent negative stereotypes and prejudices. in some cases, discriminatory practices in the workplace create major obstacles for persons with autism. children on the autism spectrum struggle to find inclusive and accessible educational environments responding to their needs. what can the commission do to contribute to tackle these problems? the commission will present this coming year a package to improve labour market outcomes for persons with disabilities. this will help strengthen the capacities of public employment services, promote hiring perspectives, reasonable accommodation and vocational rehabilitation. persons with disabilities, including persons on the autism spectrum, are among those with a higher probability of being minimum wage or low wage earners. the commission has presented a proposal on adequate minimum wages to reduce wage inequalities and strengthen incentives to work, including for persons with disabilities. the strategy for the rights of persons with disabilities will also provide accessible and inclusive education, starting from early childhood education and care, and teacher education systems. in addition, the european child guarantee will contribute to effective and free—of—cost access to key services, including childcare, healthcare and education for children in need and who have a disability. eu instruments such as the european social fund plus, the european regional and development fund, the recovery and resilience facility, the renovation wave and the technical support instrument will make an important contribution to de—institutionalisation, independent living and inclusion in the community. the commission will also foster disability—inclusive implementation of other funding instruments, such as erasmus+, investeu, and horizon europe. the citizens, equality, rights and values programme will support the work of organisations of persons with disabilities, including those focusing on autism. in relation to data collection and indicators, the commission intends to develop, at the latest by 2023, new disability indicators, supporting those in the eu social scoreboard and the sustainable development goals. we also call on member states to establish targets for increasing the employment rate of persons with disabilities. this will help achieve the 2030 employment target, proposed in the european pillar of social rights action plan and widely supported at the porto social summit. once formally endorsed by the council, the revised social scoreboard will reflect the situation in the member states, hopefully, as proposed by the commission, including the disability employment gap as a headline indicator. we also call on member states to set targets for the participation of adults with disabilities in learning. more detailed information and data on particular disabilities are available thanks to the work of eu—level organisations for persons with disabilities, supported by the european commission, including those focusing on autism. the concrete measures proposed in the strategy, and its intersectional approach, will help address the diverse situation of persons on the autism spectrum in synergy with other eu equality strategies. to that effect, we will continue to cooperate with the relevant stakeholders, such as autism—europe. this organisation, supported by the commission, released last december a report on the impact of covid—19 on autistic people and their families in europe. autism—europe also pays particular attention to the intersection of discrimination in employment and education, as demonstrated by their campaign ‘i can learn, i can work’. by supporting such organisations, the commission makes sure that persons with disabilities can share their views on eu policies to improve the situation, and take into account the specificities of certain disabilities.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, lucio moderato, an italian psychotherapist who has devoted his life to the study of autism, used words that mean everything to me: "no two autism types are alike, and so there is no one-size-fits-all intervention to implement". i'm leaving here because i care about this concept. it means that we must look at the person, his uniqueness, his subjective condition, his needs. it was, after all, the one that urged us and urges us to make the un convention on the rights, i stress the rights, of persons with disabilities. you have to bet on the person, not lock them up, not contain them. "i will never lock up my son", said gianluca nicoletti, a writer and father of an autistic boy, who is fighting to make projects fully inclusive. we need to know that every time we, who have responsibilities, imagine that we are locking up fragility, we do it because we think we are solving our problem, not that of the town or the citizen with autism and their families, who need to be helped and supported, accompanied. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council and parliament, and the council will be able to accept it. we have this huge responsibility and let's face it: in our enlightened europe, there are too many countries where families dealing with autism feel left out. from our school days, to our leisure time, to our workplace, we have to bet on two things: the commission's proposal is to provide a framework for the development of a european information society, to provide reliable reference points, quality public services and to build broad-based policies that can enhance the capacities of citizens. in particular, on the question of employment inclusion, we must start from a principle: people with autism who can work have the sacred right to do real, dignified work and not small, ghettoized, marginal occupations. we know that there are very nice stories of real work, of quality, of places where everyone can be well, but they cannot be drops in the ocean, exceptions that confirm the rule. today, in europe, about 5 million people are on the autism spectrum. many of them are discriminated against, including in the world of work, and poverty, social exclusion and school dropout are real risks on the agenda. we are in the midst of post-pandemic reconstruction and this house was instrumental in the conception and adoption of the next generation eu. so those resources, along with the others, must also go to support projects that make us make a real leap of quality in the whole area of social inclusion. the european commission and the member states therefore have a huge amount of work to do in the name of human rights and human dignity, not behind their backs.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in my speech today i want to give voice to lorenzo, nicolò, francesca, giuseppe and nico and all the other young people i have had the chance to meet in recent months, in the course of a campaign i have launched in the hearing of associations and families of people with autism spectrum disorders. we are fighting together above all to ensure that adults are protected and cared for, because, unfortunately, once they reach old age they become invisible and live in a state of total isolation. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the new technologies are used in the community. in the context of job placement, we call for individual design, which respects the individual abilities and inclinations of each person. but above all, we believe that we need a cultural revolution, i repeat, a cultural revolution, to educate about individual difference. let us remember that autism is not a world apart, but it is part of this world. we give people with autism the opportunity they deserve to achieve. it's our duty!
in writing. autism is a very complex form of disability, affecting about 5 million people in europe alone. in italy, the data on people on the autism spectrum is unfortunately growing: in fact, it is estimated that there are between 300 and 500 thousand people with autism in italy, with numbers almost double those of 12 years ago. the increase in people on the autism spectrum is accompanied by alarming data related to their employment levels. their employment rate is less than 10%, which is far below the employment levels of people with disabilities, which are 47%, and 72% of people without disabilities. it is unacceptable that these people should suffer double discrimination because they are excluded from the world of work. equally worrying is the fact that the few people employed are working in precarious and underpaid jobs. this only increases the social discomfort of this category of people, condemning them to new and multiple discriminations in their daily lives. it is therefore of the utmost urgency that the commission should act on this. ensuring employment, decent work and prospects for personal development for people with autism are commitments that can no longer be postponed.
in writing. after years of announcements, we are here again to discuss the malta agreements and the relocation of migrants. europe has been talking for years about solidarity and redistribution mechanisms, but despite the many promises and fine words, the data confirm that italy is still the one that has to host them, without europe being able to find a final solution. since the beginning of the year, 18 thousand illegal migrants have landed in italy, compared to 2000 in 2019, when matteo salvini was minister. in the last month, only 0.3% of these migrants were redistributed to other european countries, just 20 people! the european union is not a party to the treaty. the socialist governments of spain and denmark have decided to close their borders to illegal immigrants, and germany refuses to accept immigrants from italy because the number of arrivals does not justify german aid. it is time to say enough of this attitude towards italy: we cannot be the refugee camp of europe. the voluntary relocation has proved a disaster, as predicted. stopping illegal immigration, as minister salvini did, is the only solution to this emergency.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i was also missing the fact that i was also to blame for the failure of the negotiations between the european union and switzerland, because it is always the fault of the populists wherever they are to be seen. i would remind my colleagues that there must always be two people to argue, so i have obviously not yet heard a word of reproach against the european commission from the commissioner or from those who preceded me, because perhaps the european commission may also have made a mistake, who knows, perhaps we should question this dogma. i find it difficult to understand why we always reach an agreement with turkey and china and why we end up blowing up the bank with switzerland, which is a fully democratic state. we must obviously change course, because it is indeed necessary to re-establish relations with turkey, but perhaps by changing the negotiating team and changing the focus of the objectives of the european union, and finally reaching an agreement with switzerland, which, i remind you, has a positive trade balance for italy alone: we export 14 billion euros a year to switzerland and we import 18 billion euros a year, so it is true that we have relations with switzerland, but we also make money with switzerland. so it is absolutely necessary to return to having an open, clear and effective dialogue with our swiss friends.
in the name of the id group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the regulation laying down common provisions has adapted the rules on the functioning of the structural funds for territorial cohesion to the new objectives set by the commission presidency, the green and digital transition. the most critical factor in the effectiveness of european funds in pursuing cohesion, however, is well known to be the difficulty of spending in many underdeveloped regions, due to bureaucratic complexity, excessive conditionality and poor adherence to the needs of the territories. the average absorption rate of esi funds in europe is 40%, with considerable differences, with some regions absorbing only 20%. the new regulation introduced design facilities, through technical assistance, and some procedural simplifications. but, conversely, macroeconomic conditionality has been confirmed from 2023 and very strict climate conditionality has been introduced throughout the programming cycle. the fear is that the climate neutrality target, which is too close in time, will end up seriously undermining the cohesion action that is central to the treaties. time will tell whether our calls for balance and moderation, which have sadly been ignored, were well founded or not.
i would like to make a few comments, madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen. i am satisfied with the wording of the recommendation for the second reading of the common provisions in question; parliament's positions were adequately defended during the trilogues, making it possible to reach a more balanced, green and inclusive compromise. the agreement reached on the european regional development fund also brings us closer to the objective of closing the gap between the different regions of europe. i am only sorry that the constraint of economic macro-conditionality has not disappeared completely. the interreg programme is also being strengthened, with the relaunch of cooperation between regions in key areas such as health. my only regret: the decrease in the level of funding partly absorbed by brexit.

in writing. i express my strong disagreement with the report on sexual and reproductive health and rights in the eu in the context of women's health (a9-0169/2021) [2020/2215 (ini) ] which directly violates the principle of subsidiarity, attacks the sovereignty of the member states in matters of their exclusive competence and, in any case, departs from the path of respect for fundamental human rights, such as the right to life, contained in the eu treaty itself and in the international treaties. framing the right to abortion and surrogacy as part of the fight for the necessary gender equality brings the eu to the slippery slope of promoting the culture of death and turning it into the tomb of human rights: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rights of women are respected, and i would like to ask the commissioner to give us a clear answer to this question. it is necessary to show the true face of europe, the face of the dignity of every person, from conception and the consequent fundamental rights, to life and freedom of conscience.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we are all in favour of a strong commitment to environmental protection, but the climate law is a pseudo-environmentalist advert that risks severely penalising europe. your greta thunberg-style environmentalism is based on green and ethical taxes and will lead to deindustrialization, the disappearance of entire production chains in favour of non-european ones, social damage to workers and higher costs for consumers. we are opposed to your propaganda which promotes a model in which man is a threat to the environment and not a resource to protect it. behind the climate excuse you want to get to the control of lifestyles and the control of thoughts: the happy decline that you're bringing about involves taxes on plastics, taxes on sugar, taxes on fuel, and it affects ordinary people, who ultimately bear the costs. accused citizens of climate crimes, why? because they dare to have a complete diet, because they dare to have a home, because they use their car to go to work and maybe - think about it! - they also want to travel on low-cost airplanes. but how many of you are willing to give up your smartphone, give up 5g, give up cheap air travel, give up eating meat, give up air conditioning, which we have here, too? the policies you have pursued for years have made europe economically weaker than non-european countries, but without any appreciable results in terms of emissions. what you may not realize is that fewer companies that can work because of restrictive taxes and regulations mean more unemployment and more poverty. you ask european countries for ever more ambitious standards, but then, magically, we see that you also make trade agreements with asian countries that do not have these limits, nor do they have any intention of putting them in place and respecting them. your zero-emission model is a dangerous utopia. what's your dream model? the end of entire european manufacturing sectors sacrificed for chinese photovoltaic panels, chinese scooters and chinese batteries? this does not mean that pollution does not exist, nor that nothing should be done to reduce it. but we must do it on a global scale, thinking about the protection of the territories, without ideology, with an honest scientific comparison and away from the people's court created by the living room ecologists.
madam president, madam minister, mr vice-president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen, as chairman of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, i would of course like to congratulate the negotiating team, and in particular the rapporteur, jytte guteland, who has found a good compromise. it's a compromise, we all know that. parliament was prepared to go further, but with this climate law we will go two and a half times faster in the next decade than in the last decade. so, yes, of course, we have to vote on this climate bill and i have to say that the position of the green group voting against this climate bill is of major political irresponsibility, because if we all did the same, then this climate bill would not pass and we would be 40 percent of greenhouse gas emissions reduction. fortunately, we are taking responsibility. but i wanted to say, as frans timmermans reminded us, that this climate law is a start. the beginning of a long journey which will lead us to 14 july with the reform of 12 texts. and i would like to say solemnly to the european commission: do not make the mistake of extending the carbon market to heating and fuels. we had it in france, it gave us the yellow vests. you are living it in germany, it gives a toxic debate for the fight against climate change. we saw in switzerland, it led to a short majority rejecting the climate law. do not do this in europe, do not give the populists of the left and the right the argument that we will pay back part of the european recovery plan by increasing the heating bills and diesel bills of all citizens: this is politically and climatically suicidal. do not do this, it is a solemn appeal i make to you today.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european centre for disease prevention and control was set up in 2005 with the aim of creating a network for the surveillance of infectious diseases. today we are discussing the european commission's proposal to strengthen this centre by giving it new powers and tasks that go far beyond the monitoring of infectious diseases, providing for close inspections and controls in national health systems. this would mean serious interference by the european union in the health policies of the member states, a kind of commissioning of national health systems, in clear violation of the principle of subsidiarity laid down in the treaties. it is worth remembering that the primary responsibility and responsibility for protecting the health of european citizens is and must remain with the member states. in health matters, the european union has neither exclusive nor competitive competence with the other member states, but only limited competence to supplement, within well-defined limits, the action of the states. in other words, the european union has no legislative competence in this area, and can only adopt non-binding recommendations. article 17 of the treaty itself states that the european commission is the guardian. let the commission therefore be the first to set a good example. the covid-19 pandemic cannot and must not become yet another pretext for the european union to assume new and additional competences that are not its own. the national health is not touched.

why now is the time to act. this is not about diminishing the importance of the platform economy, it is about giving it a framework. first of all, it is a question of giving it legal certainty, because it is not normal that in an internal market the rules are the same in one country and very different in another, especially when there is also trade across borders. so that's the first element. the second is to give certainty, guarantee, security to those who work for the platforms. for all those hundreds, even millions of young people who, perhaps not mainly, but occasionally, and often many, work for these platforms. you have listed the different dimensions, which are first of all status. and i agree with you on that. it is not a question of imposing a status. the aim is to ensure that every worker on the platforms, regardless of their status, has social guarantees. and then, we can't be the objects of robots or artificial intelligence. our demand is that artificial intelligence has a human dimension. we cannot accept that workers are being monitored, judged, evaluated and sometimes lost their jobs simply because the algorithm is programmed that way. so we need a framework so that what is called algorithmic management is clear and that workers' rights are established, that there is transparency in this regard. so these are the issues that we're going to have to deal with. i must say to you, madam rapporteur, and to all those who have worked on this report, that you have helped us. you've identified the issues, the problems, and you've outlined the right solutions, like the burden of proof reversal, which is a tool to bring clarity and rights to everyone who works on the platforms. thank you very much, again. you know that we are practically, i would say, every day at work. today i had two meetings on this subject and the commission will come at the end of this year with a proposal which will surely be inspired by the work you have done.
the committee on transport and tourism has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. i am not sure that this is the case. for more than 100 years, workers across europe have fought for the right to a good working life, but unfortunately many of the very great gains are being rolled back. platform companies like uber, wolt and deliveroo have not only brought innovation and new thinking, they have also brought with them insecure working conditions that are mostly like something from the old days. it's hit the transportation industry like that. when drivers and passengers drive around without insurance or safety equipment, they expose themselves to serious dangers. when thousands of people are stuck in unpredictable zero-hour contracts, they lose the ability to plan their own daily lives and finances. it's just not good enough. we can do a lot better. but now the platform companies are dreaming that we are creating a so-called "third category" between employees and employers so that they can continue to avoid general employer liability. the 'light wage earner' category will be toxic to europe's social labour market model, and we in parliament must firmly reject it. we must avoid a and b teams and establish that a worker is a worker.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, platform workers represent maybe the newest form of work of the new millennium. their numbers have increased with no bounds. let me say that we are not afraid of innovations. we are not afraid of these disturbing and dystonic times in which you click a button and a pizza meravigliosa arrives directly in your kitchen. we are not afraid even of flexibility – if it is a good thing. but innovations and transformations in the labour market have to move forward with social protection – the safeguarding of the dignity and safety of workers. social rights are, at the very end of the day, human rights. we have been ambitious here. there is a three—step revolution going on. the rebuttable presumption of the employment relationship – if you have an obligation as an employee, you must have a right as an employee. there is no third way. we have put the burden and charge of solving eventual court conflicts between a platform and workers on the shoulders of platforms, of employers. finally, transparency and clear information coming from algorithms. we do not want to see any more shameful salaries in what we call a ‘democratic europe’. we don’t want to see new forms of slavery in what we call a ‘social europe’. we don’t want to see forms of labour force exploitation and abuse in what we call a ‘fair europe’. we do not ask much. we ask simply for a good balance between rights and duties, between economic growth and social compensation. we ask to build a new social, progressive, compassionate europe.

madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, there are more than 10 000 workers in italy who work on digital platforms, the so-called riders. we are talking about a turnover of 900 million euros. there are over 100 000 of them in europe, and it is a job that is constantly expanding and easily accessible to all. however, the people who are engaged in this type of work are often very young boys, they have to work very long night shifts, they are paid 2 euros per delivery, they have no sick days, they have no holidays and they are not insured in case of accidents. think about it, i'm on the road 10 or 12 hours a day. there's no easter, there's no christmas, there's no new year's, they work on saturday and sunday. sadly, because of this economic crisis and unemployment, many fathers are forced into this type of work. we must all work together to ensure greater dignity for these workers and, above all, greater rights. this must be done in accordance with european standards and the laws of the individual member states.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the vote in plenary on the report on fair working conditions, rights and social protection for workers in the platforms is an important milestone for the new forms of employment linked to digital development. in fact, work on digital platforms has raised concerns about precariousness, poor working conditions, lack of adequate social protection or difficulty in accessing it, unfair competition, undeclared work, fragmented and unpredictable incomes and working hours, lack of dispute resolution mechanisms, disqualification and lack of career opportunities, and lack of occupational health and safety measures. i must, however, point out some points which do not satisfy us in the report and i therefore ask the commissioner to consider further in future acts the invitation to the commission to examine progress in the member states in the field of social protection and the european semester. it is also agreeable to stress the importance of training, even if the commission is then asked to tackle the problem of education and training of workers on digital platforms, which is not its responsibility. the commission is also invited to consider establishing a 'european quality label' for digital platforms: what is the purpose of a european quality label, apart from going a little further? shouldn't all platforms be able to respect and guarantee the same rights? do you want to create a series a platform and a series b platform? we shall try to do our bit to encourage this, but we are comforted by the fact that history outside parliament often gives us back the reason that is denied us in this house.

in writing. i consider that a stronger fight against tax avoidance and tax evasion is of paramount importance for the recovery and recovery from the economic crisis caused by the coronavirus. that is why i support the initiatives in the report which call for an improvement in the exchange of tax information between member states. quality information is essential for effective action in member states to combat tax evasion and tax fraud. everyone has to pay taxes. for the rich, for the big companies, for those who try to escape their money to tax havens. the rules should therefore be designed in such a way as to prevent tax fraudsters from enjoying the benefits of the single market and the interoperability of borders. in addition, it is also important for sustainable economic growth and the repayment of borrowed funds that member state governments establish and maintain balanced and competitive tax systems. one that best serves their own economic interests. one that provides a sound and stable environment for investment and investment that creates jobs for citizens. europe's competitiveness also improves as member states become more competitive. the left must be reminded again and again that tax policy is a matter for the member states. instead of debates about sovereignty and powers that divide, i suggest that the left also focus its efforts on initiatives that help the unity of the union, increase competitiveness and economic recovery in the member states.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, it is clear that using spyware to hack into anyone’s smartphones is not acceptable. the expectation of privacy is a fundamental right, highly protected in all member states and by the european convention for human rights. only in very limited cases – for example, when there is a reasonable suspicion in criminal procedures – might a court, after carefully considering the specific case, justify such interference with private life. this is the rule of law standard across the european union. any indication that such an intrusion into privacy actually occurred needs to be thoroughly investigated, and all those responsible for a possible breach have to be brought to justice. this is, of course, the responsibility of each and every member state of the eu, and i expect that in the case of pegasus, the competent authorities will thoroughly examine the allegation and restore trust. such intrusions do not only lead to an interception of communication, which is contrary to the right to privacy and political freedoms, they also create a general climate of fear and distrust. honourable members, this situation is also a reminder of the need to reinforce european cybersecurity. this is not only about protecting economic activity or critical infrastructure. it is also about protecting electronic devices, such as smartphones, which have become almost an extension of ourselves and which encompass so much data, revealing both our personal lives and our professional activities. the eu is constantly stepping up its efforts in the area of cybersecurity, and it is a key priority for the slovenian presidency. there is, for example, an ongoing effort to analyse the security threats to the eu institutions in order to enable appropriate action, prevention and mitigation of risk. i look forward to the debate and your contribution today. thank you very much for your attention.
member of the commission. other madam president, mr minister, ladies and gentlemen of the house, i would like to thank you first of all for the debate this afternoon on the pegasus case, in which i am also speaking on behalf of my colleague, commissioner thierry breton, who was unable to be present this afternoon. several recent reports indicate that some national security services have used pegasus spyware to gain direct access to the devices of citizens, including journalists and political opponents. first of all, let me say that the commission strongly condemns any illegal access to systems or any form of illegal interception of user communications. in fact, it is a crime throughout the union thanks to the directive on attacks against information systems. the commission has already stressed the need to put in place a robust cybersecurity strategy and strong protection of fundamental rights such as the right to privacy and the protection of personal data, which are its main priorities. the privacy and electronic communications directive (e-privacy) guarantees the right to privacy and confidentiality of electronic communications. this directive prohibits in principle the interception or surveillance of communications without the consent of the users concerned. in addition, the directive protects the integrity of users' terminal equipment - laptops, mobile phones, etc. generally, user consent is required to store information or to access information already stored on the user's terminal equipment, with a few exceptions which are not relevant here. the european court of justice has confirmed that member states may restrict the confidentiality and integrity of communications only for strictly limited purposes and subject to the principles of necessity and proportionality, on the basis of obligations laid down by law and subject to strict material and procedural safeguards. the privacy and electronic communications regulation will further strengthen the confidentiality of communications and the protection of the integrity of end-user terminal equipment. among other things, the regulation will strengthen the enforcement powers of the authorities and increase the level of fines. it is therefore essential that parliament and the council agree very quickly on a compromise on this very important piece of legislation. in addition, processing of personal data falls, in principle, within the scope of the general data protection regulation and the data protection law enforcement directive. if the purpose is the prevention, investigation, detection or prosecution of criminal offences and the processing is carried out by competent authorities, such as authorities tasked with criminal investigations, the controllers have only to comply with all the obligations laid down in the gdpr or the law enforcement directive in addition to the e-privacy rules. data processed by public authorities for national security purposes may fall in certain cases outside the scope of eu data protection laws. in any case, the privacy of communication and the protection of personal data is always subject to strict safeguards under the european convention on human rights and the constitutional laws of the member states. it is for national authorities to demonstrate that such processing is necessary and proportionate for national security purposes. the national competent authorities are responsible for the enforcement of data protection and privacy rules under the control of the courts. it is precisely because the european union has adopted data protection rules such as the eprivacy directive, the gdpr and the law enforcement directive that the competent authorities can take action against privacy or personal data breaches. the report on the pegasus breaches, in fact, shows the need for such rules. the commission is following the matter closely. we understand that the hungarian data protection authority launched an ex officio investigation into the pegasus case on 5 august and that it has the power to investigate matters related to the secret surveillance and processing of classified information. it is expected that the hungarian data protection authority will release further information once the investigation is concluded. the normal duration of such an investigation is two months, which can, however, be extended in order to give the controllers time to answer information requests. currently the commission is taking specific measures to improve the cybersecurity of connected devices, including by way of a delegated act under the radio equipment directive which is expected to be adopted soon. the mix of measures envisaged by the new eu cybersecurity strategy, issued in mid-december 2020, will allow operators, especially critical ones, to shore up their cybersecurity defenses, with particular attention to securing their supply chain and relying on solutions secured by design. together with the new eu cybersecurity strategy, on 16 december 2020 we made a proposal for a revised nis directive to enhance the cyber-resilience of entities and networks. the nis ii proposal raises the eu common level of ambition on cybersecurity through a wider scope, clearer rules and stronger supervision tools. in addition, the commission announced in the new strategy that it would explore new horizontal rules to improve the cybersecurity of all connected products and associated services placed on the internal market. such rules could include a new duty of care for connected-device manufacturers to address software vulnerabilities, including security updates, and ensuring deletion of sensitive data at the end of life. moreover, the directive on attacks against information systems requires all member states to ensure that illegal access to information systems and illegal interception are defined as criminal offences in their national criminal law and that high common levels of penalties for these offences are in place as a deterrent effect. still on cybersecurity, let me come back to what president von der leyen put forward during the state of the union speech before this parliament this morning. she made clear that we should not just be satisfied with addressing the cyber threats but also strive to become a leader in cybersecurity. this is why we need a european cyber defence policy, including legislation on common standards under a new european cyber resilience act. protecting journalists and media professionals against online threats or illegal surveillance will be part of an upcoming recommendation on the safety of journalists announced under the european democracy action plan, scheduled to be adopted tomorrow. the recommendation will highlight the importance of cooperation between online platforms and organizations with expertise in tackling threats against journalists, as well as the role of national cybersecurity bodies in helping journalists in those kinds of situations. in conclusion, as more information is becoming available on the pegasus case, we continue to look closely into the matter. no illegal access to information or communication systems or any kind of illegal interception to users' communications or devices is allowed in the european union. the union has a robust data protection and privacy acquis, which needs to be respected with the collaboration of all concerned. we count on the close cooperation of all relevant authorities in order to ensure that all fundamental rights are respected. thank you very much for your attention, but of course i am looking forward to hearing your remarks, observations and interventions.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, mr commissioner, dear colleagues, the pegasus spyware scandal sheds light on an outrageous attack on democracy around the world. it also exposes just how unprotected are our data and privacy against malicious exploitation and manipulation. first and foremost, there is an urgent need to enforce legislation to hold accountable those who distribute, use and abuse such software for illicit and unauthorised purposes. second, it raises serious questions regarding a dark truth. we are not doing enough to ensure that the eu itself is a safe place for journalists and democratic activists. even when on eu soil, they continue to be harassed, illegally surveilled and, worse, attacked by the authoritarian regimes they sought to escape. such acts represent a grave violation of the fundamental values of our union. finally, the pegasus scandal serves as a critical reminder that we need to be vigilant toward any attack on media freedom. we in the european parliament should not hesitate to call out any erosion of media freedom, especially when it takes place in the european union, as the pegasus scandal has shown in the case of hungary. the eu must take a key role in investigating this scandal without any delay or political hesitation. commissioner, i would like to thank you for providing us with insight on what the commission is doing, and will intend to do, regarding the investigation of pegasus’s actions.
the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. we are facing a massive surveillance campaign by several national intelligence services: at least 50,000 accounts were monitored, including those of journalists, human rights activists, political opponents, and even the heads of many governments. the functioning of our democratic systems, freedom of the press, freedom of expression of activists is under attack. it is not a new danger. we have also been reporting to the commission for several years on serious abuses of this instrument, but nothing has been done so far. we cannot trust the reassurances of nso, the spyware company, that they are examining their customers on the basis of their respect for human rights, as many of their customers, such as azerbaijan, the united arab emirates, saudi arabia, show huge gaps in this field. not only that: the member states of the union, and i am thinking in particular of hungary, must explain how they have used this instrument.
