madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we too are convinced that protecting women from violence is crucial, and it is therefore inconceivable that the left should exploit such a sensitive subject to carry on their ideological battles, which have nothing to do with protecting women. let 's get this straight . the aim today is to introduce discrimination on the basis of gender identity as a minimum standard in national criminal codes. so if i say that every child needs a mommy and a daddy, am i discriminating? if i find the surrogacy to be an aberrant practice, do i have to be charged? the mechanism is clear, it's the same one that is being used in italy with the ddl zan: amend the penal code to include discrimination based on gender identity. in some countries, like mine, thousands of citizens have taken to the streets to defend their freedoms. since feminists, liberals and even some members of the lgbt community have said they disagree, then should a nice rule be dropped from brussels to make gender identity a european crime? we're not staying here! stop using violence against women or the disabled as leverage to try to muzzle those who don't think like you! while the italian parliament is legitimately escorting mr zan to the door, you are imposing the same content from brussels, which is absurd! there is even an attempt to use education as a crutch to impose an ideology on the younger generation. i'm sorry to disappoint you, but in lazio, the region where i come from, we've already blocked guidelines for schools that had the same goal. ladies and gentlemen, if we want to talk seriously about violence against women and discrimination, we are in the front line, but if the only intention is to impose ideological dictates, you cannot count on our support.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the un summit on food systems on 23 september is a historic opportunity to lead the recovery from the pandemic and achieve all 17 sustainable development goals (sdgs) by 2030. food systems, production, distribution and consumption are fundamental to achieving sdgs and therefore need to be made healthier, more sustainable and fairer. the eu has made sdgs a guiding principle of its policies, but the elimination of hunger by 2030 is currently a pipe dream, and the pandemic has highlighted the fragility of systems and accentuated inequalities. the eu must therefore take the lead in the zero hunger goal and impose a rapid transition to sustainable agri-food systems, such as farm to fork, and this question is in the right direction, but europe must also stimulate partner countries and their local farmers, fishermen and producers to switch to sustainable practices, favouring only agreements that prioritise animal welfare, the end of pesticides, respect for human rights and the fight against antibiotic resistance. we therefore ask that these objectives, which are both ambitious and necessary for a better future, be pursued.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the goal of zero hunger in the world must be achieved without over-exploiting the environment or lowering the food quality standards that we have developed over time and which we hope will spread globally. food for all does not mean poor quality food, but rather that the quantitative effort must be accompanied by an improvement in quality, in accordance with the criteria of organic and biodynamic farming. we must therefore use all the tools available to us in europe and in the developing countries, starting with precision agriculture and moving on to all the solutions using artificial intelligence with simple solutions that are also applicable to the developing countries. technology applied intelligently can enable us to overcome the quality/quantity dichotomy by providing everyone with the right and healthy food.
mr president, commissioner, after decades of steady decline, the number of people suffering from hunger started to rise again in 2015. current estimates show that nearly 700 million people, or 9 percent of the world's population, are already suffering from hunger and malnutrition. unfortunately, the world is not on track to end hunger by 2030, but i feel like it's moving away from that goal. the culprits, of course, are primarily the conflicts that are going on in many of the poorest regions, climate change, that's for sure, and the economic downturn in the poorest parts of the world, and also the covid-19 pandemic, of course. the eu, in cooperation with the other developed countries of the world, must take swift decisions and take the necessary measures to ensure food and humanitarian aid reaches the most vulnerable regions. the opportunity for change is already the un summit in september, which will also be on food systems, and i believe that the member states will be able to push through the council conclusions adopted in brussels in may this year. because to feed the more than 690 million hungry people today and the two billion more who will be added to the world's population by the end of 2050, a profound change in the global food and agriculture system is needed. i would like to highlight the important role that the eu's 'farm to fork' strategy can also play in protecting food quality, extending shelf life and ensuring food safety and facilitating food exports. it should also provide farmers with the necessary income. i therefore expect the commission to communicate how the eu can specifically help the world's hungry countries and what financial resources can be allocated to this in the eu budget. we must emphasize that it is hunger in the world that will drive migration, fuel terrorism and create social tensions. we want to prevent that.
mr president, this oral question is part of the united nations summit on food systems which will take place at the end of the month. it is clever, because it makes it possible to legitimize this summit in european circles. however, civil society actors denounced the summit as a strategy of influence by agro-industry and agro-chemical multinationals at the expense of institutions such as the special food council of the fao. ladies and gentlemen, we cannot support an attempt to take over the world food governance by the false nose of a few billionaire foundations. states, farmers' organisations and all stakeholders cannot be deliberately left out of the picture, in order to roll out the red carpet to the most risky technological solutions, but the most profitable for a few. let's not be fooled. we know that hunger in the world is not linked to a lack of production. it is the consequence of the growing inequalities. and we also know that to fight hunger effectively, we need a fair distribution of agricultural and fisheries resources and to defend food sovereignty, that is, the ability of people to control their food.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is distressing to see that the post-covid world is not on track to meet the goal of ending hunger and achieving food security by 2030. and it's even sadder to have to admit that we probably won't even reach the maternal, infant and young child nutrition targets by 2025, as set by the who assembly. we therefore strongly urge the commission to take this very disheartening and worrying fact as a starting point, now that we must plan our support for a global transition to fair and sustainable food systems. women - i say this to the commissioner who knows this but must inform the whole commission - and in particular women in urban areas, who make up 43% of the agricultural labour force, are therefore the indispensable and indispensable actors in the fight against hunger and malnutrition, and the primary responsible for the growth and development of children and children. especially in response to the covid crisis, eu funding and programmes should now ensure that food security strategies always take a gender approach on the one hand, and implement concrete measures for children on the other: the commission has already made a number of proposals.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen: 'sovereignty at home, charity in the south'. that is the european food strategy in a nutshell. sovereignty is the basis of a resilient society. food self-sufficiency is the minimum level of food requirements to which all countries in the world should be entitled. similarly, eradicating world hunger by 2030, as the un puts it, is not an option, but an obligation. but these objectives will remain unattainable as long as the european union continues to undo with one hand what it claims to be building with the other. officially, the union works for the good of humanity, but in practice it encourages its farmers to export en masse, with subsidies, especially to the south. a strategy that helps eradicate the local food-producing agricultural fabric and organizes dependence on our well-staged charity. i would like to know, commissioner, how the commission intends to reorient its trade policy to support the global transition to sustainable agri-food systems, in line with the 'farm to fork' strategy.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the food question is absolutely central to the achievement of all the sustainable development goals. the way we produce, distribute and consume food inevitably determines what the world of tomorrow will be, its environmental and social sustainability. this is why it is tragic to see - as ms toia said earlier - that the path to a healthier, more sustainable and equitable global food system is now sorely delayed and that the goals of eliminating hunger by 2030 are far off, in a context where covid has clearly accentuated fragility and inequalities. there is also - and this is a good thing to remember - a strong connection between human rights, land and sustainable food systems, which puts people who live off the land at the centre and who are trying to defend it from the wild exploitation of resources. i believe that the union must stand by them with the utmost clarity, including by putting in place concrete instruments to defend their rights and their ability to live in dignity.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the economic contraction caused by the pandemic has had a dramatic social and employment impact throughout the european union, especially in certain economic sectors. the current regulatory framework is clearly insufficient: the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a welcome step towards the creation of a european employment strategy. the sure instrument itself did not guarantee coverage for all categories: for example, workers in the digital platform industry, which has seen a huge expansion in the last year and a half, do not have the same protections as other categories of workers. on the one hand it creates opportunities by reducing barriers to entry into the world of work, but on the other it raises serious concerns about the conditions under which they work. in addition, the effects of the pandemic have led to huge disparities between categories of workers, both employed and self-employed, and between permanent and fixed-term contracts. the consequences of the pandemic have unfortunately been situations incompatible with the levels of social well-being achieved over the years in the countries of the union, such as the endless queues of citizens in front of the entrances of humanitarian associations in order to secure a hot meal, despite the fact that a few months earlier they enjoyed more than decent incomes. it is the commission's primary task and duty to monitor these inequalities and put in place effective corrective instruments to eliminate them.

mr president, commissioner schmit, first of all i would like to congratulate you on your strength and conviction at this late hour. the covid pandemic has so far caused more than 750 000 deaths in the union and has tested every cog, every piece of the social cog and of public policies, amplifying the crises, the threats, the challenges of our time. all of us who have been here, talking to him, asking him, have talked about a lot of things, but it's almost unfathomable. so what we have to conclude accordingly is that, at the very least, we have to strengthen the pillars of public policy for the future, because some of the things that are happening and that have happened must be absolutely unrepeatable. i'm just going to mention three: first, we can't ignore the impact of the virus on the most vulnerable, our seniors, in the early stages. as hard as it is to look back on this, we have to address triage so that it never happens again. secondly, in the case of people in residential care for disability, for age - for whatever - we have to study the absolute situation of emotional isolation so that something like this can never happen again. thirdly, as regards children, there is something very urgent that we must do: children from disadvantaged backgrounds in technological poverty have been excluded from the education system and it is now urgently time for the member states to do something to prevent this kind of failure in school. so, commissioner, force and the bull.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the resolution sets out topical issues and objectives on which specific impact assessments are essential for the impact of this initiative on the lives of citizens and future generations worldwide. it is true that the loss of biodiversity could cause irreparable environmental, social and economic damage. in fact, about 75 per cent of the genetic diversity of plants has been lost, while 75 per cent of the world's food is generated by just 12 plants, and this poses a risk to global food security. we wonder whether we should speed up the approval process for new breeding techniques, to make other species more resilient and help maintain biodiversity. according to the latest research, these goals are not contradictory or conflicting. the joint research centre has studied the impact of the specific objectives of the european biodiversity strategy and has shown that the food needs of the union citizens would become more dependent on imports from third countries, resulting in increased emissions and loss of biodiversity from third countries, all to satisfy european diets. we therefore ask that an impact assessment be included with this document, so that we can consider the requests we make to other countries as well.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, when we talk about artificial intelligence in law enforcement, it is not just the question of facial recognition, a delicate issue on which a balance needs to be struck. hackers and criminal groups across europe are too easily able to break into the computer systems of public institutions and private companies. they have affected health facilities in italy, france, germany and spain, for example, putting their functioning at risk. artificial intelligence must become a fundamental tool in the hands of law enforcement to combat cybercrime more effectively. but it is not just the suppression of criminal phenomena. much more needs to be invested in prevention, by strengthening the defenses of personal data that may otherwise end up in the data black market. we need to create effective barriers against hackers and use artificial intelligence as a sort of infiltrator, so that law enforcement can block possible attacks before they happen. prevention is much more effective than cure.
in writing. artificial intelligence is set to play a strategic role in effectively fighting crime and ensuring the safety of citizens. the opportunities and benefits that it can offer in criminal justice are indisputable in terms of the prevention, investigation, detection and prosecution of criminal offences, provided that the compatibility of technologies with respect for fundamental rights and the principles of necessity and proportionality is ensured. forms of mass surveillance and predictive policing through sophisticated algorithms that judge social behavior or personality traits to anticipate the behavior of certain subjects do not shield us from the risk of opaque decision-making, or worse, from new-generation lombrian theories and dangerous rights violations. a common regulatory framework at european level is essential to facilitate law enforcement authorities in the use of artificial intelligence services and tools that meet criteria of transparency, impartiality and equity, avoiding prejudice and discrimination between individuals or social groups, risks of violation of privacy and human dignity.
in writing. the pace of digitalisation, technological developments and, in particular, ai tools, has been outpacing legislative work in this area for many years. however, given the ongoing digitalisation of the internal security landscape, we cannot afford to hold back the development of new technologies. the use of new solutions must be adequate and proportionate, while ensuring, on the one hand, a high level of protection of fundamental rights and, on the other, the conditions for law enforcement agencies to use analytical and statistical solutions that effectively support the objective of ensuring the safety of citizens. the commission should be empowered to adopt delegated acts in accordance with article 290 tfeu concerning the establishment of a european data protection supervisor, the appointment of a european data protection supervisor and the appointment of a european data protection supervisor. in the context of this report, the specificities of law enforcement agencies should be taken into account and their needs should be met, where possible, without prejudice to the protection of fundamental rights, the maintenance of which is a priority. it would be justified to separate the issue of the use of ai tools in systems supporting the implementation of tasks in the field of internal security from the draft regulation on the use of ai and to regulate it in a separate legal act. further development of effective analytical, statistical tools is necessary in the face of new challenges and threats, but only while maintaining a high level of protection of fundamental rights.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is my honour today to introduce, with my colleague, the rapporteur, mrs kontoura, this debate on the own-initiative report on the impact of domestic violence and custody rights on women and children. the vote we are called upon to cast today as the european parliament is of great importance, and i believe it is our duty to send a strong, clear and hopeful message to all the women who have suffered and still suffer physical, psychological and economic violence at the hands of their partners and who are struggling every day for their own survival and that of their children. domestic violence is a serious and often hidden social problem, causing systematic physical and psychological trauma, with serious consequences for the victims and a serious impact on the emotional, economic and social well-being of the entire family. the victims of this stigma are not only women, who suffer firsthand from the mistreatment of their spouse, but also children who often witness acts of violence against their mother, with serious consequences for their psychological and emotional development. we condemn in the strongest terms all forms of domestic violence and violence against women and call for justice to be provided for women and children, particularly in cases of separation of spouses and parental custody. in too many cases, domestic violence is still overlooked, and court decisions on shared custody or parental authority can result in death sentences for women and children. this is also because criminal proceedings arising from a domestic violence complaint are often handled completely separately from civil proceedings for separation and custody. national judicial systems should also have a college of professionals and experts who can be directors in judicial proceedings involving minors and women victims of violence, to ensure that they are treated in the most appropriate manner and to avoid further harm to victims. but all of this must start with a necessary revolution in terms of community awareness and training and education against violence in all settings, and this cannot be done without community and national support. to this end, too, we are calling on the commission and the council to add gender-based violence to the list of areas of crime under article 83 (1) of the treaty on the functioning of the european union, taking into account the particular need to combat this crime on a common basis. with today's vote, ladies and gentlemen, we have an opportunity to show all women who are victims of violence within the walls of their homes that they are not alone, that the institutions do not abandon them, but rather actively support them. this fight is not political, but it must see us all united in the supreme goal of protecting fundamental rights, gender equality, security and justice.
member of the commission. – mr president, at the last plenary session, i addressed the high priority of tackling gender-based and domestic violence, in particular in the light of its increase during the pandemic. both the gender equality strategy and the eu strategy on the rights of the child put a strong focus on eradicating violence and ensuring that all women and children can live free from violence. the victims’ rights strategy pays special attention to ensuring that the most vulnerable victims, such as child victims and victims of domestic violence, can fully rely on their rights to support and protection. for this protection, it does not matter where in the eu and in what circumstances the crime took place. children are affected by gender-based and domestic violence committed within their family in many ways. some children suffer from physical, emotional or sexual violence that is aimed directly against them. some children suffer when witnessing such violence aimed against one of their parents or siblings. some children may also suffer from violence in the context of joint custody and visiting rights, when such rights are awarded to a violent parent. indeed, the failure to restrict custody or unsupervised visiting rights may result in tragic consequences, both for the other parent and the child concerned. all such children suffer in one way or another from gender-based and domestic violence. all such children are victims of this terrible crime. we need to protect all victims, and in particular children, no matter how they experience violence in their families. they may not even be aware that violence is being committed in their families, yet they may still suffer the consequences. domestic violence has been shown to negatively impact the physical, mental, emotional and behavioural development of children. it can also have lasting effects on health, well-being and prosperity well into adulthood. we fully agree with parliament that in order to keep our promise to children, to protect them and to ensure their well-being, we must combat all forms of gender-based and domestic violence. we must provide adequate support to children to prevent that the consequences of the violence they experience impact their future. this is exactly what we are doing. the revised brussels iia regulation, applying as of august 2022, reinforces the rights of the child in cross-border custody cases by ensuring that they are heard and their perspective is taken into account. the new rules ensure a more effective cooperation between central authorities, courts and child protection authorities, or other competent authorities. the central authorities are the direct interface with parents and should continue playing a key role in supporting courts and competent authorities in applying the regulation. in addition the gender equality strategy announced new legislative measures to achieve the objectives of the istanbul convention within the limits of eu competences. in line with parliament’s request, the proposal for a directive on gender-based violence against women and domestic violence will propose measures to prevent such violence, including when perpetrated online, to protect and support victims, improve their access to justice and ensure better coordination between competent authorities. the proposal will contain targeted measures aimed at criminalising specific types of conduct, in particular with regard to cyber violence. it will furthermore step up victims’ rights, in particular on compensation, prevention and protection for victims of domestic violence, including children victims and witnesses of such violence. all this can be done with the existing legal basis. in addition, we will present an initiative inviting the council to include hate crime and hate speech in the list of euro-crimes set out in article 83. this would subsequently allow the commission to propose criminalising misogynistic hate speech and hate crime at eu level, a request which you repeat in this report. we are confident that the upcoming initiatives will achieve our shared objectives and we look forward to cooperating with you on these initiatives.
on behalf of the ppe group. ladies and gentlemen! violence occurs in the homes of the educated and the uneducated, in the homes of politicians, doctors, police officers, and manual workers, in rich and poor countries. the lack of a quick, adequate response from the relevant authorities, as well as frequent turning of the head or simply social approval only discourage the perpetrators of violence. that's why it's so hard to fight. the statistics are not encouraging, and the covid-19 pandemic and the lockdowns have definitely intensified and shown the scale of this phenomenon, which mainly affects women. many countries do not have effective anti-violence programmes, and not all eu countries have ratified the istanbul convention. we also hear about attempts to speak out against it, despite the fact that its writings have directly contributed to supporting victims of violence in many countries. let us not forget that where there is violence, children are also victims. the very fact of witnessing violence leaves a mark on a child's psyche and later life. that's why it's so important that we don't wait, that we react. victims of domestic violence in each member state suffer in the same way, and violence has a negative impact on entire communities. october is domestic violence awareness month. so let's all show some empathy today, support the report, and do everything we can to fight this phenomenon. and i repeat, without the understanding and support of men, we will continue to reap the negative fruits of domestic violence.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. these are the rumors, mr president, that accompany the case studies of the so-called "parental alienation syndrome", as daniela poggio, a feminist and pas researcher, has pointed out. and the desperation of these children is hanging over them, keeping them from sleeping, from thinking of anything else, and it makes us wonder how it is possible that in europe, the home of the rule of law, in the name of a supposed syndrome rejected by the scientific community, mothers and children are being pushed away. it is also called "malignant mother syndrome" or "symbiotic mother syndrome", just to leave no doubt that this supposed syndrome is fueled by very serious gender biases. in italy, lawyers teresa manente and ilaria boiano of differenza donna counted 100 cases of children removed from a parent for this reason from 2018 to 2020. in 8 out of 10 cases, the mother was blamed for the rejection of the other parent; in 9 out of 10 cases the child was never heard by the judge; in 100 cases out of 100, in all cases, the mothers have been intimidated, their freedom has been restricted, they have to keep silent and they have to suffer the cruelest of all the violence: the so-called "maternity ablation", that's what it's called. they must be silent in the face of the compulsory treatment of children who are removed by public force and taken to neutral facilities, where they must be reeducated and re-aligned, in this case, obviously, yes, manipulated. mothers must remain silent even when they are treated as dangerous criminals and instead are uncensored citizens and, in most cases, victims of partner violence. but if it were possible, ladies and gentlemen, to draw up a list of the victims of the pain and harm that this established custom in the courts inflicts, children would be at the top. because they are deprived of childhood, because they are deprived by the force of their mother. is that acceptable? we think not, and we have reiterated this in this text. i now turn to parliament, which must choose whether it will stand on the side of civilization, the rule of law, science or barbarism.
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we have always been strong supporters of the protection of the family, of women and especially of children, and i commend the rapporteurs on the passion with which they presented their initiative. but we must be very careful, given the delicate nature of the issues involved, not to fall into the mere generalized demonization of man on the basis of a distorted view of society. in fact, we believe that it is a grave mistake to project the image of a totally patriarchal and misogynistic european society, even suggesting some legal solutions that can be used with the intention of upsetting the relations between men, women and children within the family as a whole. some paragraphs in the report invade the powers of the member states in matters of criminal law and family law in a completely misleading way, so that in our view it is better to rely on the national sphere of competence. we must fight all forms of violence in every way possible, but we must not allow ideological considerations to interfere in the fight against violence in order to influence legal and criminal action. we must continue to fight for the protection of women and children in the appropriate places and the question of child custody is too serious to be subject to a priori assessment.

in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, petitions have always been a means for all european citizens to participate in community policy. petitions are a useful means for people with disabilities to make proposals for their rights. through petitions many disabled people have recently contacted us, telling us what problems they face every day, for example difficulties in employment, difficulties in school, difficulties in accessing public offices, difficulties in getting on public transport. i am very glad that our proposals have been accepted in this text. however, the implementation of the european charter on disability needs to be accelerated. we must fight all forms of discrimination in the workplace. we need to help families support people with disabilities. we must support schools and institutions that house children and young people with disabilities. it is our duty to tackle the problems of disability with all the means at our disposal, providing support and help to those less fortunate than ourselves.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, i regret to say that there will be no real protection of the fundamental rights of disabled people until their status is mutually recognized among the member states of the european union and until there is a cultural revolution in favour of difference. without this crucial step, all our efforts to ensure that the countries apply the community provisions correctly will be in vain. the european disability rights strategy 2021-2030 therefore calls for the full implementation of the european disability charter by the end of 2023. we must now extend this pilot project and make it compulsory, together with the european parking badge for disabled people. only then will we have a strategic tool to help people with disabilities exercise their right to free movement in a europe without barriers, in a europe without exclusion.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the target we have set for 2050 is ambitious and unfortunately we must remember that we have not achieved the 2020 target of halving road deaths and understanding what weaknesses need to be corrected. some countries are lagging behind in implementing the measures to be taken, and the cost is borne by the weaker sections of the population, the elderly in particular. we still have a lot to do. first of all, we need to focus on prevention, which comes through education, training, securing infrastructure. we need to upgrade infrastructure and adapt it to a new idea of road safety that takes advantage of technological developments and new ways of moving around urban areas. i conclude by saying that it is time to rethink human mobility, putting people at the centre of policies. we still have a long way to go. it's time to start.
mr president, commissioner, i have to ask you to leave. ladies and gentlemen! every year, nearly one million road accidents occur in the european union, claiming the lives of some 23 000 people. in my country, poland, last year alone, nearly two and a half thousand people lost their lives on the roads. despite significant improvements in recent years, the scale of the problem remains enormous and frightening. therefore, further intensive actions to improve road safety are needed. realistically, i know that we may never be able to eliminate road accidents completely. however, we must make sure that accidents, if they do occur, do not result in death or serious injury. to this end, action is necessary, on the one hand, to improve the quality of the road infrastructure, which should maximise the safety of road users. on the other hand, we must ensure that vehicles on the road are equipped with technologies to assist the driver in safe driving. i am pleased that from next may, additional safety systems such as intelligent speed assist or advanced emergency braking systems will have to be installed in new vehicles, which i hope will help to reduce the number of accidents.
mr president, the human factor is now the main cause of road accidents in the european union. alcohol and drugs and speeding, often combined, are responsible for more than half a million accidents in the european union each year. we need zero tolerance for these offences, we need to remove the users of these substances from the road, we need to strengthen training, we need to focus on digitalisation, and a productive link between infrastructure and vehicles will make the roads safer. these are the regulatory and financial bets that the union can make on road safety, which needs better indicators and a special effort in the cities. better protection of pedestrians and cyclists is urgently needed to safely promote more sustainable mobility. bilbao, which was awarded by the commission for being the first european city to introduce the thirty kilometres per hour limit throughout the city, has prevented one in three of the accidents on its streets in one year, thanks to this and other measures. i think it can serve as an example.
member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank you and the honourable members for this debate. i have taken good note of your comments and will pass them on to commissioner vălean. the commission is confident that, with the continued support of the european parliament and with the buy-in of member states, we will be able to deliver on our road-safety commitments for the decade ahead, whilst ensuring that the framework remains, as ms kountoura puts it, fit for the future.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place on tuesday, 5 october 2021. written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. the rapporteur notes in her report that there are about 1 million conflicts in the eu every year. in 2019 alone, there were 23,000 deaths and 12,0000 serious injuries. although the eu and its member states have made considerable progress in road safety over the past decades, these figures are particularly worrying. road safety must be a political, social and personal priority. moreover, these current figures reflect future tragedies resulting in huge costs to society. this is why the eu's long-term vision is to achieve the best possible approach to zero road deaths by 2050 (vision zero). our fundamental right to mobility should not be accompanied by deaths from road accidents and this principle should be incorporated into all road safety decisions.
in writing. there is an important problem with truck drivers coming from outside the eu. as you know, for the most part, they don't comply with the driving ban of more than eight hours a day without rest. on the roads of bulgaria, romania and germany, we have seen many times that sleepy drivers, mostly of turkish origin, have caused road accidents, and unfortunately, in most cases, they have caused fatalities. as the resolution says, the highest percentage, more than half, of road accidents happen on rural roads. unfortunately, there are whole regions in europe that are not included, and unfortunately i see no intention on the part of the commission to include them in the development of the ten-t network. of course, i will also mention the former corridor number 8, which links the bulgarian ports of varna and burgas with the italian ports of bari and brindisi via the albanian ports of drach and vlora. i cannot fail to mention mobility i, we want to reduce road accidents, but at the same time we are deciding to move hundreds of empty lorries from one end of europe to the other, just so that certain members of parliament can fulfil their election promises made to their union constituents. to me, that's hypocrisy to say the least.
in writing. statistics from recent years show that the number of eu citizens killed or seriously injured in road accidents is still high and shows a stagnating trend across the eu. on average, 500 people are killed and 300 seriously injured in the eu every day, far from the european commission's 'zero vision'. hungary has also managed to achieve good results in the field of road safety in recent decades, with statistics showing a downward trend year on year. however, every serious road accident is both a personal tragedy and a loss for society as a whole, and active action is therefore needed to further improve road safety at member state level. the state of infrastructure and vehicles contributes significantly to the development of road safety indicators. therefore, steps should be taken to ensure that modern driving aids are equally accessible and affordable for union citizens in all member states. i therefore ask the european commission to support more technical developments in the automotive industry and to support member states' efforts through both regulatory and financial means to make new accident prevention technologies easily available and affordable to the widest possible range of new cars, regardless of price category.
in writing. the issue of road safety concerns us all, as about 1 million road accidents occur every year in the eu. the numbers of deaths on the asphalt are certainly shocking. in 2019, there were 23,000 deaths and 12,000 serious injuries. the union's targets of a 50% reduction in road accidents by 2030 and a 'vision zero' for zero road fatalities by 2050 can be achieved with a specific strategy, political will, increased and targeted enforcement measures and adequate funding. the road safety report lays the foundations for safe infrastructure, responsible drivers and road safety education. in this context, we support lower speed limits in the urban area, zero tolerance for drunk driving, as alcohol is responsible for 25% of fatal road accidents, and more safety measures in infrastructure and vehicles. in conclusion, i would like to stress the importance of investing in the education of those who drive on the roads and in the training of the new generation of drivers, which is a priority for improving road safety and behaviour. all member states must ensure that high quality road safety education is provided, starting at school and forming part of a continuous lifelong learning process.
the next item is the report by mr raffaele stancanelli, on behalf of the committee on fisheries, on the recovery of fish stocks in the mediterranean: assessment and next steps (2019/2178 (ini)) (a9-0225/2021).
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for their availability and for the contribution they have made, allowing the report to be adopted unanimously in the committee on fisheries, demonstrating that the sea and the fisheries sector are a priority for everyone. the report aims to underline that the proper implementation of the common fisheries policy should have as its primary objective the achievement of the right balance between economic, environmental and social sustainability. the mediterranean sea has supported the coastal populations and economies of the countries bordering it for millennia. it is also one of the most exploited seas, with levels of up to 80% for certain demersal species. however, there are other environmental factors underlying the decline of specific fish stocks, as fisheries and their ecosystems are subject to sources of pressure and degradation, such as pollution, climate change, hydrocarbon exploitation, dredging and shipping. the commission's willingness to set high environmental standards is entirely acceptable and positive, but this should not be at the expense of increasing imports and reducing food production in the european union. in recent years, some countries have seen fleets and crews halved, with a decrease in production of over 50% in favour of imports, which currently stands at around 85% for small pelagic and demersal species. the conditions must be created so that the conservation of resources and the profitability of enterprises can coexist and go hand in hand, so as not to frustrate the efforts made to date. it is necessary, in the general interest and also in order to safeguard resources and the profitability of undertakings, to harmonise all regulations in order to simplify fishing activities and not to place additional burdens on undertakings. it is essential that, more than one year after the entry into force of a regulation, scientific research on the health status of the stocks concerned is carried out immediately in order to obtain scientific data on the effectiveness of the restrictions applied and to verify their suspension. greater involvement of stakeholders in decision-making is vital and the key points should be, firstly, greater involvement and co-management of stakeholders and, secondly, complementing scientific data with the knowledge of industry stakeholders. taking environmental data into account alone risks overlooking some very important aspects such as the socioeconomic implications of fishing restrictions. ensure the rule of law. the conventions and treaties signed by the countries bordering the mediterranean are numerous, not only for the management and protection of fisheries, but in practice the actual implementation is extremely diverse and has concrete contradictory elements. the conditions for real and effective cooperation and collaboration in the management of fisheries in the mediterranean are clearly established and exist, so as not to make the efforts required of the european union fleets useless, limiting fishing activities and consequently also economic sustainability. in this context, the united nations convention on the law of the sea, signed at montego bay, states that each state is free to determine the breadth of its territorial waters up to a maximum of 12 nautical miles. some states arbitrarily apply more than doubled limits, creating situations of absolute danger and making the coasts of countries bordering the mediterranean subject to encroachments by vessels from other countries, which go up to three miles under the coast. the conclusions we can draw: a holistic approach that takes into account all aspects affecting the state of health of the sea and the constant updating and collection of scientific data on the state of the stocks. sustainable fisheries means ensuring the sustainability of the entire fisheries sector and the entire production chain, with positive socioeconomic implications.
in writing. the link between the united states and europe, even before it is political, military or commercial, is a cultural and historical link, which has its roots in the years when europeans, english, germans, french, italians and many others, came to america and shaped a new society, but always based on european and christian culture and tradition. over time, this cultural consonance has intensified political relations between washington and the european states, which have always seen the united states as their first trading partner and, above all, as a loyal military ally. this is why the eu-us friendship and alliance must look beyond political administrations and focus increasingly on the global challenges we will face together: the european union is also concerned about the threat of communism and totalitarianism in china, the dangers of global terrorism, the regional ambitions of the islamic regime in iran, the risks posed by the taliban in afghanistan. only together can we meet these global challenges, thanks above all to the mutual guarantee of our security, the atlantic alliance, the indispensable pillar of transatlantic relations.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, there was a mistake in the debate this morning. let me explain: the union has increased sanctions and restrictions on the prohibition of overflight of airspace and access to european airports for carriers in russia. the consequences are often tragic. the sustainability of adoption programmes, which traditionally involve european adoptive families and belarusian children, is compromised. once adopted, children are forced to stay in their own country, physically separated from their parents who are unable to visit these children except through occasional visits. it is clear that these occasional visits cannot replace the normal operation of adoption programmes, which should instead allow adopted children to live permanently with their new family. it is a situation detrimental to the stability of these children who, having lost their natural families, are also deprived of the opportunity to build a stable bond with their new parents. three weeks ago i sent a letter to high representative borrell and i am still waiting for a response. the european union must also be able to take action to ensure that the rights of children are respected and that the rights of children are protected.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. i would like to thank you very much for mentioning the more than 700 political prisoners in belarus. let me give you an example: ksenija syramalot sentenced to two and a half years for helping to organise a peaceful demonstration for free elections, for democracy, for human rights. i took a sponsorship for her. and i ask you all to follow my example and also sponsor those people who have defended western values, democracy, free elections in belarus. and secondly, to the commission: we now have sanctions regimes. yes, we must ensure that the circumvention of this sanctions regime cannot take place in this way. and i ask you to consider extending sanctions to the raw material wood. the forest in belarus is almost 100 percent state-owned. the timber is sold internationally and brings foreign exchange directly into lukashenko's treasury. and so we directly continue to finance the oppression of the population. please consider adding wood to the sanctions list.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, for more than a year we have been witnessing a dramatic deterioration in the human rights situation in belarus. about 700 are now political prisoners, including the main opposition and free voices in the country. nearly 5 000 criminal proceedings are pending against those citizens who peacefully demonstrated against the falsified election result and for their fundamental freedoms. the union has not stood by and watched this unacceptable repression, but has also imposed just sanctions. we must show firmness, we must continue, we must not back down in the necessary protection of human rights. the humanitarian situation on the border between belarus and poland is extremely worrying and it is also true that belarus is making a heavy use of migrants. here is europe - and i must say this very clearly - and it cannot in any way abandon those who are fleeing such dramatic situations.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the european health emergency preparedness and response authority (hera) is a key instrument in strengthening the european health union and an important first step towards the european health union which we have been calling for for some time. the pandemic crisis has shown all eu countries that health threats, such as covid-19, do not affect borders or borders. i can therefore only express satisfaction at the creation of an authority such as hera, which will improve the union's preparedness and response capacity to serious cross-border health threats and will make the necessary countermeasures available and distributed rapidly. it is clear that this authority must be operational as soon as possible, but without this implying a reduction in the prerogatives of parliament, which has an essential power of democratic control and scrutiny vis-à-vis the other institutions. the response to health threats, as this pandemic has shown, must be concerted and the european institutions will have to continue to work together in the broadest spirit of collaboration and with respect for their respective roles.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, thank you for that very, i would say, intense discussion. i will try to address as many of the questions and concerns that you have raised as possible. i started noting down each mep to answer to every one of you by name and directly, but there was a great deal of overlap so if while answering i do not include all of your names, please excuse me for that. several of you raised the point of exclusion of the european parliament. i have said it before and i will say it again: in order for the european health emergency preparedness and response authority (hera) to deliver on its mission in case of a new pandemic or a new health crisis, we needed to ensure that it had both the powers and the financial means to do so, and the only way we could do this was under article 122 of the treaty. article 122 is the only legal base which allows the union’s assistance to be granted to member states when a member state is in difficulty because of exceptional circumstances beyond its control, and this necessitates a proposal for a council regulation in order to enable hera to take a series of actions, including financial support triggered in a time of crisis. in the preparedness phase, hera will rely on normal budgetary procedures and the european parliament will of course be deciding on hera’s budget but furthermore – and i have already said this – the european parliament will have a seat on the board of hera. there will be an open, frank and systematic dialogue on all of hera’s operations. i also wanted to tell you today that i have already written to the chair of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi) inviting the nomination of the board member. why did we set up a commission service and not an agency? three reasons. speed. establishing of an agency would have taken too much time. it would have taken a minimum of two to three years of interinstitutional negotiations. money. the multiannual financial framework negotiations have just been concluded and there is no specific budget line for a new agency, and by establishing hera as an internal commission service we were able to make use of existing budget lines such as eu4health, and you had such a crucial part to play in this and enabled us to have this budget – horizon europe and the union civil protection mechanism. thirdly, agility. because the existing models of european agencies do not allow agile decision- making by virtue of their legal character. i also wanted to tell you here that the commission can easily redeploy staff and mobilise funds in case of urgency and this is why, i think it was meps zacharopoulou and rivasi who raised this point. meps fritzon, guteland and others raised the point of what we wanted to achieve with hera, whether it is falling short and more. hera builds, i believe, and institutionalises our successful approach. it is all part of the vaccine strategy. we are proposing a complete end-to-end solution, from a very early identification of a threat to ensuring that the appropriate medical countermeasures are available for our citizens. and let’s not forget that hera will be looking at all medical countermeasures, including therapeutics and diagnostics and even personal protective equipment. vaccines are just one of them. hera will finally be able to better coordinate and ensure coordination between the eu and the national initiatives on crisis preparedness and response. this is something totally new and an area in which hera can make a real difference. there was a question, i think from mep sofo, on the relationship between hera and industry. i wanted to tell you this, and i think it is very important, hera will be improving the union’s development and manufacturing procurement of key medical countermeasures but to do this, hera will need to have the preparedness mode functions as well as the capacity to take part in emergency measures so it is going to be working across all sectors – research, academia, ngos, governments, and, of course, also with industry, because industry is a key sector in terms of medical countermeasures which are needed for health preparedness and health response. is it going to be a prolonged arm of the pharmaceutical industry? we will be working very closely with industry, including the pharmaceutical companies. this is inevitable. i think all of you that have worked from the very first day of this crisis know this. however, the role of hera will be to guide industry and pharmaceutical companies into the directions that are needed to safeguard public health and hera will ultimately, fundamentally ensure that public funds have public returns that serve to improve the health security of citizens. so, with hera we are engaging in a new type of public-private relationships that will benefit eu citizens and the european parliament, member states and all the eu agencies will be very, very closely involved throughout the process. several meps raised the issue of the european centre for disease prevention and control (ecdc) and the european medicines agency (ema): why do we need a new agency, would we not be stealing staff? no, we will not be stealing staff. we will be recruiting new staff and we will be working with member states’ experts and a memorandum of understanding in terms of how to govern the cooperation/collaboration between ema, ecdc and hera will be signed but i wanted just again to say that hera will be supplementing with added value to the work already conducted by ecdc and ema, both in preparedness and in crisis times without overlaps and in full respect of the mandates of both agencies but compared to ecdc and ema, hera will have a stronger anticipatory and forward-looking and responsive-focused dimension in terms of threat assessments and foresight, and will be able to scan the horizon for medical countermeasures. ecdc will remain the key actor in terms of risk assessments for infectious diseases, which is at the heart of its mandate, and ema on the scientific advice on the safety and effectiveness of high-quality medicinal products, but ema cannot engage with the private sector on the procurement or development or medical countermeasures, so there will be a very close cooperation, but there will not be an overlap. meps trillet-lenoir and auken spoke about transparency, and i believe this is a word that often comes up. all meetings will be subject to the applicable eu rules on transparency, of course, and public procurement and reporting. it goes without saying that hera’s budget and expenditure will be subject to the discharge procedure in the european parliament, which will remain the budgetary authority. hera will not be able to spend money without your approval, and we will maintain a privileged dialogue with the european parliament, which has a permanent seat on both the hera board and the crisis board. i apologise for taking up more time, but i want to go through as many questions as i can. how do we have a proposal for which the european parliament has no envisaged role? first of all, i want to thank you for the support you had given at the beginning for the european health union proposals, for the support you had given to this project that has become hera. but let me be clear: the hera proposals do have a role for the european parliament and we look forward to working very closely with you. we could not have done this without you, and the commission will invite parliament to propose and set up a review process to ensure that you are closely integrated in the process and agree on its outcome. it is, though, a different structure, like i said it at the beginning, meps weiss and guteland raised it, and i want to say this: because of the need to ensure a coordinated approach at eu level, both for preparedness and response, we decided to create a special board to steer the work of hera and monitor its implementation. this is something which is highly unusual. it is not something we normally do. we faced a number of legal constraints. member states are responsible for their own actions. we do not intend the hera proposal to challenge the treaty provisions. we cannot weaken the institutional prerogatives of the commission, nor for the implementation of the eu budget. we consider that the hera board should include representatives of the national administrations and a representative of the european parliament. parliament’s representative – your representative – will have the full capacity to contribute to all the discussions and to assure a direct link between your political and legislative work, and i believe that this is important for the success of hera. meps weiss and liese asked about the sunset clause and whether we could introduce it in the proposal. i have already said that a review of hera is foreseen and the results of the assessment will be shared with parliament and council and if justified, a subsequent proposal for a different second generation of hera could be elaborated, building on the experience we have already gained. last but not least, i want to come to the comment – i would say it was a question, but it was also a plea – from mep ştefănuță, on the situation in romania. we stand by to support every single member state in every single way we can. i cannot agree with you more that we are all in this together and we need to work together to increase the vaccination uptake. this is a challenge in many countries and we need to do this if we are going to be able to target the pandemic effectively. i will speak with the mep again to see how we can further support to romania and these efforts. i want to thank you for the openness today. i listened to all your comments carefully. i hope that you were able to also hear our responses to them. i take on board and understand a little bit why we put forward the proposal of hera the way we have. our approach has also always stemmed from a very honest intention to respond immediately, considering the threats that surround us and the gaps that have been identified, and i can just say one thing, that we are open to change if necessary. we have a clear willingness to get this right, but we cannot afford to find ourselves at the start of the pandemic, never again, and personally i will stand in this plenary as many times as i need to, to build the level of trust and transparency that you rightfully demand and that we all believe in.

in writing. the spanish socialists advocate the creation of a european authority to prepare for and respond to health emergencies, as well as to reduce our dependence on health. the pandemic has highlighted the eu's lack of access to relevant medical countermeasure stocks, the vulnerability of supply chains and the lack of a coordinated and systematic approach to their development, production and procurement. in this regard, an eu authority would address the structural gaps in the eu's health preparedness and response capabilities with regard to biomedical development, production and development of response capacity. the socialists, however, regret the limited role given to parliament in this great project. during the pandemic, parliament has shown its cooperation, involvement and speed in negotiations and decision-making, so its mere observer role in the crisis committee is disappointing, as is the legal basis used by the commission for the procedure, which excludes it from functioning and decision-making. the socialists therefore ask that parliament not be left out of the new authority.
in writing. the pandemic has shown us how important cooperation between member states is to protect the health of europeans. while we've had good results in fighting the pandemic, we're not sufficiently prepared for other potential crises. the recovery from this pandemic is an opportunity to build stronger and more resilient health systems. we need a mechanism that can be activated if the infrastructure of some member states cannot cope with crisis situations beyond pandemics. to ensure access to medicines, we need joint procurement. we also need better cooperation on treatments for rare diseases. but we will not be able to overcome these challenges unless we lay the foundations for a more sustainable model of health cooperation that is better prepared for crises. hera is a good step forward and will prepare interventions in potential health crises by gathering information and building the necessary response capacities. we need much more and that is why we must continue to work to build a true european health union and unlock the immense potential of health cooperation.
in writing. two years ago, when we first started talking about creating a european health union, we didn't expect it to go this far. the strange positive effect of the covid pandemic is that the goals that were set then have now become the result. the coronavirus pandemic has clearly demonstrated that global challenges can only be addressed through collective solutions: this is especially true for healthcare. it is misleading to say that member states are able to provide adequate solutions to these crises within their own sphere of competence: more union means better healthcare in this case. the steps and initiatives taken by the european commission so far are all important steps forward, but the pace and content of the european health union cannot be fully satisfactory; little attention has been paid to the urgent need to address inequalities between public health systems. the european parliament has already urged member states to implement stress tests that show the functional and qualitative weaknesses of their health systems; however, so far, member states have not implemented them. on the basis of the results of these tests, the european commission should on the basis of a resolution adopted by parliament initiate the establishment of a uniform system of quality criteria to define the standards to be ensured in european supply systems. the creation of the european health union is not just about protecting against covid. since the european community is built on solidarity, inequalities and injustices in supply systems must be addressed without delay! other
the next item on the agenda is the debate on the report by milan zver, györgy hölvényi and janusz lewandowski, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, the committee on development and the committee on budgets, on the report on the implementation of the eu trust funds and the facility for refugees in turkey (2020/2045 ((ini)) (a9-0255/2021).
the rapporteur. - madam president! - i have to go. the report we are discussing today is a joint venture of three parliamentary committees: afet, deve and the budget. each of them is entitled to their own views on such important foreign policy instruments as the four trust funds and the facility for refugees in turkey. thanks to the excellent cooperation with the co-rapporteurs györgy hölvényi and milan zver, whom i thank. thank you, too, shadows. thank you to our advisors. we have found common ground and that is why this report was adopted by a large majority in the joint committees. this report is more than a glimpse into the past. it contains vital clues for the future. we appreciate the role of trust funds as a tool for rapid response in emergencies, in humanitarian disaster situations. they allow blending they allow the european budget to be combined with the member states' and other donors' funds, and they have also fulfilled their mission. that is why, among other things, their life was extended until the end of this year to allow for a smooth transition to the new neighbourhood development international cooperation instrument. the approval was conditional, namely that it did not entail additional contributions from the eu budget this year. while appreciating the role of the trust funds, the european parliament has repeatedly criticised the way in which they were set up, extended and, above all, committed, often without consulting the european parliament, often at the last minute, even though they had serious implications for the budget of the european union. a perfect illustration of this is the new aid package for syria. the fact that syria, the syrian refugees, are still in need of help was well known to the european commission last december, during the so-called budget concession, but it was not reflected in the 2021 annual budget or in the 2021-27 multiannual financial framework, or even in this new ndici instrument, so the most important conclusion of our report is this: a new generation of programmes, new instruments must be launched with the involvement of the european parliament from the very beginning. today we are closing, we will soon be closing the european union chapter of trust funds, but we all know that there can be extraordinary circumstances that will require extraordinary measures, perhaps also in the form of new trust funds. but their launching is a last resort, must be duly justified and, above all, must fully respect the prerogatives of the european parliament as the budgetary authority.
the reporter. madam president, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would first like to thank my co-rapporteurs, mr janusz lewandowski and mr györgy hölvény, and all the shadow rapporteurs for their good and constructive cooperation in the preparation of the report on the implementation of the european union's rescue funds and the facility for refugees in turkey. politically and operationally, the trust funds have brought a new quality of cooperation to the field of the european union's external action. there's no doubt about that. the european union, which often lacks visibility and effectiveness in this area. the eu administration is often criticised for being slow and inadequate in providing aid. well, eu trust funds were set up precisely for this purpose, to provide the possibility of swift and effective action. after reviewing the implementation of the trust funds, i would highlight, in particular, the flexibility, proactivity and rapid response to real needs on the ground and the possibility of third member states participating as donors and enhanced political dialogue with partner countries as one of the key advantages of trust funds in the eu. as the existing trust funds will cease to exist at the end of this year, we need to focus on the many lessons learned from the implementation of trust funds and use them to improve the implementation of the new generation, as my predecessor has already spoken, of the external financial instruments of the european union, in particular the ndici europe in the world. i would stress the need to instrument of public diplomacy, and i am talking about the ndici, and the european parliament has a key role to play in this in the future. focusing on a brief overview of the colombia and madad trust funds and the facility for refugees in turkey, for which the afet committee is responsible, it is clear that they have justified their added value, fulfilled the objectives set and largely achieved the expected results. the war in syria, which has created the largest refugee crisis in modern history, is having a devastating impact on syria and the wider region. the madad trust fund and the refugee facility in turkey have been important tools in providing humanitarian and development assistance to syrian refugees and their host communities. i think it is extremely important that programmes under the funds support the most vulnerable groups in particular: children, women, young people, and protect them as much as possible from violence, abuse and exploitation. the eu trust fund has enabled children in jordan, lebanon and turkey to attend school and have access to safe spaces for non-formal education, protection and psychosocial care. the funds in trust also provide better access to health services and support for increased economic opportunities and social inclusion. special attention shall also be paid to vocational training, which improves the access of syrian refugees to the labour market and to better income. if we look at the situation in colombia, we find that it remains complicated. the country is facing multiple crises simultaneously that are undermining efforts to reach a peace agreement. violence remains widespread, with high levels of organised crime and serious human rights violations. the colombian trust fund has indeed managed to achieve tangible results in providing development support to small farmers and smes, thus making a significant contribution to local development in the regions most affected by the conflict. respected ! unfortunately, the needs and challenges that led to the creation of these trust funds in the european union are still present. the covid-19 pandemic has worsened, internationally, and it has also caused a deterioration in the international security environment. this requires the efficient use of the full range of financial assistance instruments provided for in the current multiannual financial framework. the european union must strengthen its leadership on the international stage and ensure a stronger, more autonomous, more unified and more decisive foreign and security policy to ensure a rapid resolution of crises and the maintenance of global peace and stability.
we now continue our debate on the report (a9-0255/2021) by mr zver, mr hölvény and mr lewandowski, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, the committee on development and the committee on budgets.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, i’m happy to attend today’s debate. i would like to express our appreciation for the effort of the committee on development and the committee on budgets of this house for this report and the continued cooperation in this field. the commission considers the eu trust funds and facility for refugees in turkey as a key financial and political instrument that has allowed us to respond to the unprecedented refugee and migration crisis around europe in an effective manner. we think they have delivered. they permitted faster decision-making processes for leveraging additional resources and for increased coordination and impact. but even more so, they brought the practical and tangible results. the trust fund for africa was designed to respond to the diverse risks of instability, irregular migration and forced displacement. thanks to the trust fund for africa, irregular arrivals to italy and malta have decreased from over 180 000 at the peak of this crisis to 35 600 in 2020 and around 44 000 so far this year. we have saved many lives. the trust fund for syria has mobilised over eur 2.3 billion since 2014. on top of directly reaching 7.8 million beneficiaries, it will have a sustainable legacy in helping refugees and local hosting communities. it has been key to implement eu pledges, and it has allowed the eu to respond rapidly and flexibly to the challenges of covid-19 and the beirut port explosion. the facility for refugees in turkey is unprecedented in scale and reach. it has made a major contribution to contain the flows of irregular migration, provide basic needs to refugees and enable a faster government scale-up of health and education services. these are just some examples, and i’m glad to see that the report shares the overall positive assessment of all these tools and recognises their added value. let me also address two observations that the report raises. first, we take transparency as well as monitoring and evaluation very seriously. since the inception of the trust fund and the facility, the commission has kept the relevant committees of the european parliament well informed about progress achieved in the implementation of these programmes. we have done this through annual reports and certified annual accounts, as well as through a monthly financial report on the four trust funds, produced by dg budget. the european parliament was also invited as observer in the strategic boards of the trust funds and has been regularly kept informed about programmes adopted by the operational committees. the second that i would like to touch upon, in the respect of human rights, is a clear objective of our trust fund throughout different regions and countries, which the commission takes very seriously. we are in close coordination with the implementing partners of our programmes and external experts that are conducting regular project monitoring missions to make sure that human rights are respected in the implementation of these programmes. to give you an example, the european commission has put in place a rather unique third party monitoring of operations in libya. it is paramount that we make the results of the trust funds and the facility sustainable. while the measures taken by the eu and member states have brought down the overall number of illegal border crossings in recent years, developments on some routes give rise to concern and require vigilance and urgent action. in order to prevent the loss of life and to reduce pressure on european borders, the eu needs to keep taking solid actions for priority countries and routes in close coordination with member states and our partner countries. in this regard it will be essential also to secure adequate and predictable funding, as has been the case in the past six years. the commission remains fully committed to addressing these challenges, and i hope to count on the continued support and cooperation of the european parliament. i look forward to discussing all these issues with you.
the rapporteur of the committee on budgetary control. madam president, mr commissioner, yes, what we want to say with this debate is that when friends are going through a crisis, we need to help them. it is said, "a friend is known in adversity". it is very good that the european union has special trust funds through which we can help those who are in crisis today and those who are most in need. these are definitely not symbolic amounts. in the case of turkey, we have sent about six billion euros to help refugees, and it was the european union that did one of the biggest pieces of helping to solve the syrian refugee crisis. but what needs to be said is that we need to have oversight and control over money. and that is what we have always advocated in the committee on budgetary control. you can't just give someone money on a plate and not ask them what they did with it. we need to talk to our partners and ask our turkish partners where the money went and what the benefit of the money is, what the purpose is and how we will continue to work with these people and who the beneficiaries of this money are. yes, a lot of things have worked out, but we need to keep going and we need to keep channeling this money to those who really need our help. and finally, let me thank those who used this money and helped to get it to the most vulnerable, which were women, children and the elderly.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, european values oblige us, bind us to solidarity, to de facto solidarity and to the uncompromising defence of human dignity. the solution for refugees has to be a european solution where all member states work together, where all member states cooperate. in addition, the financial resources we provide must be transparent and must be within the budget of the european union. i regret to say this, commissioner, but there is at least one member state which has received european funds for refugees, which has withheld them, and which has not made them available properly. it is necessary that this should not happen or be repeated. the european commission also needs to be more proactive. the european commission knew that the refugee problem would continue, even worsening with afghanistan. in the negotiations on the multiannual financial framework it did not put forward any proposals to support refugees. and now, an important point: refugees are not to blame. refugees cannot be used as an excuse to cut funding, programs and policies that are essential. and we can't give the populists an argument. refugees are victims. refugees are not to blame and it is our duty to help them.
