madam president, ladies and gentlemen, today we are voting on a fundamental act on the use of european trust funds and the refugee fund in countries such as central africa, syria and turkey. we are talking about the relationship between europe and migration flows, between europe and rights, between europe and reception. so, if we are to speak in depth about rights and democracy, let us say loud and clear that we can no longer accept blackmail from countries such as turkey or, more recently, belarus, which use migrants as commodities or as cattle to be moved or moved here and there according to their national interests. we say very strongly that we cannot accept that the countries receiving the funds use europe as an atm machine, that respect for the fundamental rights of the people is not optional. we must say emphatically that we cannot accept that the objectives of development, employment and education should be subject to the logic of power and the horrendous exploitation of human beings. and we say very strongly that we cannot accept european funds to finance the libyan coastguard, perhaps to bring hundreds of migrants back to the port of tripoli and send them to detention centres where there is a disregard for human life. we, the socialists and democrats, are calling for the establishment of an independent monitoring mechanism to monitor the use of these funds. parliament cannot be shut out! the rights are not for sale, "les droits ne sont pas à vendre": ursula von der leyen said it, let us say it loudly too!

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, in europe 10% of households live in conditions of energy poverty. 2.7 million workers cannot afford to heat their homes. this is an alarming social and health emergency, which is likely to get worse with rising bills and the ongoing covid crisis. that is why we must act immediately, with emergency measures at european level, perhaps investments and facilities in the field of housing. we are a long way from the ecological transition envisaged by the european union, which sets unrealistic targets and forgets the issue of energy poverty altogether. we cannot ask the member states of the european union to give up natural gas and then tie ourselves to the technology and raw materials of third countries such as china, which certainly contributes to global pollution much more than europe. we are not going to stand by and decide to stand by all those families and businesses that risk being left behind, sacrificed in the name of a blah, blah, blah, light years away from that social dimension that europe is so full of.
commissioner, as we are having this debate, every business in bulgaria is paying 170 euros for a megawatt hour of electricity. unlike other governments in europe, the bulgarian government is doing almost nothing to soften the blow to business and citizens. the fact that the price per megawatt hour is three times higher than usual, and that many governments, like the one in bulgaria, are not responding, is not just a national problem, however. the first voices against the green transition and against the idea of a european energy market are already being heard. the fact that governments like this one in bulgaria are doing nothing will also affect the opinion of many europeans about the european union. but the european commission is not doing enough, keeping record high emissions prices in europe. we need, right now, a flawless response from the european union. besides acting now, we have to make sure this doesn't happen again. we need a system of energy stress tests in europe that measures how the economy will respond to different set prices for gas, oil and electricity. the commission should also be able to provide information on the impact of the measures taken on the environment and the environment protection. we should also launch an annual assessment of the european union's energy security, similar to the credit rating of the banking system. i hope we all realize how big the risk is for europe right now.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. mrs. simson, you said prices will stay high this winter. but 'heating or eating' must not be the alternative for people in europe this winter. yes, last year gas prices were very low, but that's no consolation today when we see a partly rapid increase since may 2020. commissioner, the measures taken by your authority are urgently needed, and in fact overdue. but the short-term protection of people from high, even unaffordable, energy prices is entirely in the hands of the member states. the european institutions are primarily responsible for medium and longterm measures. the rapporteur has already made some suggestions on this. i would like to emphasize once again that europe will never be energy self-sufficient. we can produce a lot of sustainable energy, but the demands on households, on mobility, on decarbonization of industry are enormous. so if imports are to remain unavoidable, then it is necessary that we diversify our supply to avoid dependence on supply and prices. so let's start with a climate diplomacy to north africa for building renewable energy there and producing green hydrogen.
i have to say, madam president, it's become more expensive to have the lights on in this room and to have the microphone on in this room. and when it shows up in people's bills, it causes european politics to be disturbed. what can we do to prevent energy shortages from driving prices skyrocketing? i understand that the commission is considering a solution whereby we are to buy up more natural gas and store it. but rising energy prices must not be used as an argument for the need for more fossil fuels. nor must it make our political colleagues shake hands in the transition to renewable energy. on the contrary, the rising bills should be an important reminder that we need to invest even more in more wind turbines, solar cells and much more electricity infrastructure. one of our biggest climate challenges for 2030 is to get everything we can to electricity. that is why we must increase the production of renewable energy today, not tomorrow, so that we do not run out and have to go back to expensive natural gas. it is also the only way to ensure that the price of green energy becomes more and more competitive and that europeans avoid the big extra bills.
madam president, i would like to thank you. let's start with the basics. an economy built on finite resources will always lead to sharp price increases when those finite resources begin to run out. it's as simple as that. the increase in energy prices today is due to the fact that the supply of fossil gas is lower than the demand. that demand is currently rising sharply in asia, and in russia, for example, the supply of fossil gas to europe is now being reduced. that is why energy prices are rising, not european climate policy. that said, we cannot turn a blind eye to the challenges facing parts of europe today, especially those with low incomes and poor access to cheap renewable energy. therefore, eu investment in the green transition must be specifically targeted at those parts of europe. the green transition must therefore go hand in hand with a fair distribution policy and social reforms. we need a big increase in investment in renewable energy, energy efficiency, increased transmission capacity between parts of different countries, but also between the countries of europe. and we need to invest in energy storage, like hydrogen and batteries. that's the solution to rising energy prices, not increased reliance on fossil fuels.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the rise in the cost of energy is killing off businesses and households in europe, but this is only the beginning if the environmental question is not cleaned up by the ideological violence of the left. the race to the dark side of the energy transition risks damaging both economic development and the environment. and there's worse: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. while we are debating, russia is playing its game and communist china, which is the country with the highest co2 emissions in the world, is building new coal-fired power stations to resell energy to europe at ever-increasing prices. and it is still china that has a monopoly on the raw materials for the production of electric batteries for the storage of renewable energy. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, if by banking union we mean the transfer of supervisory powers from the national authorities to the european ones, it is not clear why there is still such strong discrimination. asymmetries are found to the detriment of commercial banks which essentially operate in support of the real economy, i.e. to the benefit of the productive system, and to the advantage of those which operate predominantly in financial transactions not supported by commercial transactions, but which nevertheless, being exposed to very significant risks not properly monitored and codified, can adversely affect the entire banking system at a systemic level. these fears stem from the awareness that there are potentially high risks to which many european banks have been exposed for years now, having in their assets complex financial derivatives, i.e. those categorised in level 2 and 3 and the so-called toxic securities, the risk of the other derivatives being already presided over by the ccp (central counter party) and emir (european markets infrastructure regulation) on otc derivatives. the commission notes that the bank's capital needs are not limited to the capital needs of the bank, but are also limited to the capital needs of the bank. it is therefore incomprehensible that, while restrictive and codified guidelines have been adopted to deal with the commercial problems, the accumulation of enormous risks in complex derivatives, left to the sole self-referential calculation by the banks themselves, with macroscopic conflicts of interest and without assessing the risk of the growing phenomenon of the shadow banking system, where there is still no adequate supervision, has been inexplicably protected. the whole european banking system is also affected by the uncertainties of basle iv, the details of which will be known at the end of october, but there is a real risk that its implementation will further penalise small and mediumsized enterprises with a still fragile credit system. finally, as regards the completion of the banking union and the common edis deposit guarantee scheme, we are not convinced by a hybrid model for the common guarantee fund, because without a real and genuine mutualisation of risks it will not produce any advantage, nor will it strengthen the capacity of the union's banks to withstand any shocks. on the contrary, it will emphasize the principle of the risk differential between central and peripheral economies and, paradoxically, will pave the way for those who advocate the abolition of the sovereign risk free.
on behalf of the id group. mr president, in the matter of asylum, it would be reasonable to finally return to the strict definition of refugee as contained in the geneva convention. i quote: 'any person who has a well-founded fear of being persecuted on account of his race, religion, nationality, membership of a particular social group or political opinion'. but we are seeing hundreds of thousands of refugees today who claim to be fleeing war, but they are coming mostly for economic reasons and to benefit from our social systems, which are very beneficial. as for those who are denied the right to asylum, they are hardly ever expelled and therefore remain on our soil. asylum has become a separate immigration channel. the creation of this european asylum agency, which you are calling for, will only have one result: to send a new signal of weakness and laxity, that of a europe whose doors are wide open. lucides, several leaders of member states are warning you: we must not repeat the mistakes of 2015. in the last six years, five million asylum seekers have flooded our continent, the equivalent in number of the inhabitants of the greater east region, almost the population of denmark. denmark, let us talk about it, is a country to take as an example! copenhagen has already withdrawn asylum from allegedly syrian refugees, as there is now no reason why they should not return home. what we need is not an asylum agency, but an agency that will organise, in a humane but effective way, the return to their country of origin of all those who have nothing to do in europe. your european pact on migration and asylum is at a standstill. to rise to the challenges, courage is needed, as in lithuania: detention for illegal migrants who have not made a legal asylum claim; as in hungary: building a wall on the external land borders of the european union; the european union is also a major donor to the fight against poverty. as for belarus, we need diplomatic and economic sanctions against countries that refuse to take back their migrants or that threaten us. the objective must be clear: to stem the flow of migrants and stop the rush to europe.

this means that the rules on the union are binding only on matters for which the states have delegated their powers to brussels by means of the treaty, but they are not binding at all on national constitutions and legal systems, much less on matters on which the individual states have never delegated their sovereignty to the union, and in this case there is no exception to those aspects which govern justice. it is therefore strange to raise a debate of this magnitude, not least because it is not the first time that some national courts have questioned the primacy of community law. it happened in italy, it happened in germany recently, you will remember, with the purchase of securities by the central bank, or with what was the suspension of the ratification process of next generation, in italy happened in 2017 with the taricco case; so this means that the recent legal history of the union is littered with similar cases, but i don't remember such heated debates or fears that germany or italy would leave the union. i would like to conclude, ladies and gentlemen, with a warning, because sometimes the excessive desire to punish, which many of you are carrying out today, leads to a violation of the treaties. that would be a violation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, i think that the agreement reaches an important step forward. firstly, multilateralism indexation matters demonstrate that it can deliver, and this is very important. and secondly, for the first time, we will be establishing at global level a minimum floor on tax competition, which is, indeed, very good news. but i also have to say that the agreement leaves a bitter taste in some of the aspects. 15 percent – we know it’s too low. and here we have to regret the role played by some also in europe, for instance, to take out the words ‘at least’ before 15 percent. secondly, the thresholds are too high. this will mean that those new rules, pillar one and pillar two, will affect only a very limited number of companies. and thirdly, the way pillar one is designed will mean that the revenues of the new mechanism will not go to countries that are more in need and where more activity actually happens. so it leaves a bitter taste, even though we recognise it’s an important step forward. now the question is what we do at the european level, and we fully agree that this agreement needs to be swiftly implemented as soon as possible. and here there are things where greater ambition cannot be put, but there are areas where we can actually, as europeans, put greater ambition. nothing prevents us to change the thresholds actually at european level to put more ambition on a deal. and here is, i think, something that the commission should look at, because this agreement is a very first step, but we need to go much beyond what has been agreed at the oecd level.

the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud and tax evasion. however, there are still many countries, from fiji to panama, that do not have cooperative jurisdictions for tax purposes. so what is the point of the proposed global code of conduct? the following on the proposed global tax rate of 15 percent: taxation is a national competence and tax competition is one of them. the tax foundation has shown that corporate tax is mainly borne by employees and consumers. up to 70 percent of the tax burden falls on labor. when the us lowered the tax rate, it was demonstrably good for investment, jobs and wages. the global minimum corporate tax is not so much about ending tax competition between countries, but rather about ending the competition between the wishes of progressives and reality.
mr. president, i have to ask you... i am pleased that a compromise has finally been reached on the reform of the global tax system, which hungary was happy to join. i am proud that, despite the enormous pressure, we have been able to take a determined and consistent stance and actively shape the agreement on the international stage. i think our most important result is that the corporate tax rate in hungary remains at 9 percent and the agreement does not impose a new burden on manufacturing companies. the devil, however, is in the details. i agree that the relevant eu legislation should also be developed to ensure consistency with eu law and uniform implementation. i hope, however, that the union does not intend to shoot itself in the foot. if we want to catch up with china and the us, we must not lose sight of the issue of competitiveness. fair tax competition is a good thing, tax harmonization would kill it and force companies abroad. so let us not introduce stricter rules in the union than those contained in the global agreement. finally, we must not forget that this is a matter of national competence. consensus shall be sought, respecting the position of each member state.
mr president, the first thing i want to express today is a great sense of achievement. one hundred and thirty-six countries have finally reached an agreement to end the deleterious race to the bottom caused by deregulated international fiscal competition. this agreement will help us fight fiscal elusion, fiscal dumping and will provide new resources to many countries that can be used to address social inequalities and to support economic recovery. someone says it’s not enough. yes, many of us would have loved to see a more ambitious agreement. but now we have two choices. we can keep complaining and despise the agreement, or we can acknowledge the enormous effort made, and we can push for a rapid and effective implementation of the agreement in the european union as soon as possible. we choose this latter option and we call on all the parties involved at the commission, the council and every member state to take a step forward and to facilitate such fast and strict implementation. now, more than ever, we need more tax justice. let’s get started.

has certainly been influenced by the aggressive campaign of politicians and the media over the last year. the other side of the argument is of course the ngos, who accuse the frontex agency of not mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank the rapporteur for her work. we in our group are often critical and caustic even in our criticism, but we recognize when a job is done well, and above all when it is done for the benefit of the european citizens who benefit from these agreements. we can say that we are basically satisfied with the result, because the scope of the legislation has been clarified, for example, so that electric vehicles and electric scooters are also being regulated, although this could be expanded a little. in italy there is a debate on the regulation of insurance, for example, of scooters or anything that does not have so-called muscle traction. everything with electrically assisted traction should be regulated in some way, including insurance. the increase in the protection of injured persons, including in the event of accidents involving vehicles from another country, with foreign vehicles, is also important. for those of us from border areas like me, these are things that unfortunately happen quite frequently, so that's another important point. we say that the most important thing about this measure, however, is the protection of citizens, especially with regard to the harmonization of minimum amounts, as well as the protection of guarantees for companies that fail, which in any case allow european citizens to find compensation, to find satisfaction at the moment when they are victims of an accident. the damage must be compensated, the damage must be satisfied. as regards the work on reducing road casualties, there is still a lot of work to be done, but i am sure that we will all be actively involved in this.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the crisis we have been witnessing in the last few hours on the polish-belarusian border is yet another attempt by a non-eu country to destabilise europe with a hybrid threat. the european union is facing a blackmail, a blackmail to which europe is responding, a blackmail to which europe is looking on, not deciding, but judging, by those who are promoting the illegal trafficking of migrants. the threat, like that in brussels, of infringement proceedings against member states which defend their borders, like poland, only serves to encourage criminal business. the defence of europe's borders, whether they are with belarus, between greece and turkey or in the mediterranean sea, is the priority to be achieved by any means, including the erection of walls. the images coming from the border show thousands of migrants massing, arriving after paying, behind the false promise of being able to enter europe. and you are accomplices! we hope that brussels will take decisive action, with concrete measures to prevent illegal border crossings; we call for greater cooperation with third countries, particularly in terms of readmission and prevention of departures, by linking the receipt of economic and development aid and the conclusion of visa agreements to them. unfortunately, in this house, we often choose to speak from only one point of view, that of the left, always ready to point the finger surgically at legitimately elected governments which are not to their liking. today's is just one example. we stand with poland, with those who decide not to submit to blackmail, always on the side of the european citizens. if europe is truly united, it should do something instead of just watching.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, on 9 november 1989 the berlin wall fell: it was the triumph of freedom and the final fall of a totalitarian system that oppressed the peoples of the east. since then great strides have been made by countries that were formerly crushed under soviet influence. today, 32 years after that day, the wall must be erected, not to isolate us but to protect the peoples of europe from the blackmail of foreign powers. in an attempt to emulate erdoğan's provocations, today the belarusian dictator lukashenko exploits migrants in order to blackmail europe as his turkish counterpart did. the poles have already paid a lot: first with the nazis, then with the communists. our firm resolve as europeans must be to defend poland as we have failed to do in the past and, consequently, to protect our land. in recent weeks, on several occasions, some people have spent hours in this chamber questioning poland, a piece of european history. it is time that everyone understood that the security of our citizens depends on that side of our continent and that it must be defended at all costs.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i am starting from the assumption that lukashenko's action is of course to be condemned without reservation. president charles michel announced the opening of a discussion on the financing of physical barriers against migrants: but what are we talking about? we're talking about immoral acts, not illegal ones. they keep talking about hybrid war against the european union: it's very clear to me that people should not be exploited, but we absolutely do not play their game! we put people first. these are human beings, not enemies to defend ourselves against. we're talking about people who are being violently and brutally rejected. people crammed on our borders, left out in the cold with no food and no care; people who are not allowed to apply for asylum. i have no other words but one: shame on you! shame on you! 15,000 police were deployed against innocent, unarmed and unarmed people. not even if we were at war! stop talking about war, let's talk about a humanitarian emergency and let's live up to the founding values of this union: the european union is a union without walls, a union of rights, a union without hatred!
in writing. what is happening at the border between poland and belarus is extremely serious. as erdogan has done in the past, the belarusian dictator lukashenko is using migrants to threaten europe in a new form of hybrid warfare right on europe's border. with this threat, the issue of border protection has finally returned to the agenda in brussels, gaining a central place in the political debate. border protection and security, especially for those states on europe's borders, such as poland and italy, are of crucial importance to tackling today's geopolitical challenges and to avoiding the threat of illegal waves of migration fomented by dictators from third countries. the security of not just the member states but of the whole european union is at stake. we need decisive action from brussels to secure it and defend our borders.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, problems of press freedom exist even in countries which are regarded as the bulwarks of democracy. the case of assange is emblematic and the european institutions should take a more decisive approach to the problem. in the silence of the media themselves, there are many journalists forced to defend themselves against reckless lawsuits, made only to hinder their work, which is the basis of the right of citizens to be informed. journalists have already been subjected to searches, interceptions or investigations to find sources and block their reporting activities. parliament has done its part with today's excellent report, and we now expect an ambitious legislative proposal from the commission. the cooperation of the member states is also needed, because we can no longer hear stories of journalists like those of report, lorenzo tondo or alfio caruso, who are brought to trial and sometimes even condemned - in situations of obvious conflict of interest - simply for telling the truth.
madam president, commissioner, i think we have already discussed this subject. without freedom of the press and freedom of speech, we cannot say we have a democracy. but we all know that in the crisis, the pressure on the press is even greater, and so in the two years since we've been in this pandemic crisis, if you look state by state, you'll see that a lot of journalists have suffered. they are not allowed to express, for example, and to show to the citizens - because after all the press informs the citizens - cases of illness or certain documentaries, documentary films from specialists from other states. this is an example from my country and we have to say, yes, there is subsidiarity, the member states have regulated the functioning of the press through institutions like we have the national audiovisual council, but they are given fines every day, every week, for abandoning. that is what 'the punch in the mouth' means, and i believe, commissioner, that we, parliament, have now done our duty. i think you should come up with a proposal for a uniform regulation at the level of the european union because, after all, journalism is becoming cross-border.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, i think that the debate showed that we are dealing with a very important, urgent issue and that we are speaking here about a very important report, which i am really thankful for because it is the report encouraging the commission to take action and to prepare the package that will contain legislation and non-legislative measures. and i take a very clear message from this debate that justice must not be, or continue to be, instrumentalised against freedom of speech. the commission is determined to put on the table a very solid anti-slapp package that could be adopted by the co-legislators speedily. we need these rules on the ground without any further delay. i would like to recall that this initiative is part of a wider effort to protect media freedom and media pluralism. unfortunately, we see developments which raise really very serious concerns. together with increasing threats to their physical and online safety, legal threats and abusive litigation add to an environment where hostile activity against journalists is growing and can have a serious impact on their willingness and ability to continue their work. this was also underlined in this year’s state of the union speech of the commission president, and this is why we address the different aspects. as a first step, we have recently adopted a recommendation on the protection, safety and empowerment of journalists and other media workers, but there is more to come. work is ongoing to prepare a european media freedom act to safeguard media freedom in the eu. your report also refers to these two initiatives. with the european media freedom act, we aim to increase transparency, independence and accountability around actions affecting media freedom and pluralism. the situation of media in europe is getting worse, and we have never done so many things as in this mandate. and so i think that this debate also, as i said, is very encouraging for all of us to continue the work, and i am sure i can count on your sustained support.
rapporteur. – madam president, dear colleagues, thank you very much for this debate and i think the message from the european parliament today is clear. we encourage the european commission to come forward with an ambitious proposal. commissioner, thank you so much for your words. i really just wanted to underline, in your recent rule of law report, you also covered strategic lawsuits against public participation (slapps) and i think it is really important to highlight the danger of these lawsuits, so i am really happy that you already covered it. but parliament really encourages you today to be ambitious. there have been a few exemptions, but they are the obvious ones. i think it is really clear that we as the european parliament stand behind journalists, behind scientists and we stand up for press freedom and the rule of law. i would like to thank all colleagues for their support, and i would especially thank my co-rapporteur roberta metsola for this very good cooperation. we worked together very well in a spirit of defending civil society activists so thank you so much, and i would also like to thank our teams and advisors who really intensively worked on this report. today, i am proud of what we achieved so far, and i hope that we will continue this and will have a successful package in the end to protect journalists and ngos from slapps. thank you very much, and i hope to see you with the for the final package in a few months.
rapporteur. – madam president, i should like to thank everyone for taking part in this debate. thank you to tiemo and all our shadows for their excellent cooperation and shared purpose. thank you, madam commissioner, for your commitment on this issue. thank you, vice-president. the ball is now in your court to propose laws that truly make a difference, and there is the blueprint here for europe to be a safe space for journalists. together, we have an obligation to preserve the fourth pillar of our democracy when others simply want to chip away at it. so let me end with a short appeal: what we've heard today shows us how important this issue is for us. it is important institutionally, but also personally. this is for daphne caruana galizia, who was assassinated with 42 slapp suits pending against her and her bank accounts frozen before any judgment was handed down. this is for ján kuciak and his fiancée, who were murdered for reporting facts. this is for all those media houses who had to choose between funding a court case or paying their staff. this is for all those aspiring to be journalists in our europe. for them and for us all i would urge you to vote in favour of this report tomorrow. let this house show them that they can truly rely on our protection.

in writing. – media freedom is fundamental and we must do our utmost to safeguard and protect free independent media across the eu. in the last years, shocking assassinations across the union have reminded us that while democracy strengthened with time and technology, freedom of expression remains under attack. the rule of law and good governance are our union’s fundamental values. malta and the s&d strongly share the same aims and objectives in safeguarding the protection of journalists. i’m glad we are today discussing the report by two of my colleagues, proposing eu-wide anti-slapp legislation; i’m more satisfied that malta is at the forefront of introducing its own anti-slapp legislation, part of our media and defamation act, which i believe will be in line and go beyond, in some areas, what will be enacted at eu level. in the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs (libe), i focused mainly on widening the protection to artists, academics and civil society - who shape opinions within our society and strengthen our democracy. they deserve the same protection. look at the work by the atlas of hate in poland. court cases initiated against young artists for simply outlining the municipalities declaring themselves ‘lgbt-free-zones’. we must be on their side too.
in writing. when they joined the european union, all countries accepted the union's fundamental values, which include not only the principles, norms, institutions and rule of law of liberal democracy, but also freedom of the press. these points, as well as many others, are about minimum wage, corruption, inflation and i could go on and on hungary is a military power. but let's get back to freedom of the press. the orbán government and its foreign allies are systematically reducing the media market until only their own media remains on the market, not only in hungary but also abroad. journalists are being wiretapped, their work is being compromised, newspapers are being bought or closed. meanwhile, orbán is pouring millions into his own newspapers. today, viktor orbán personally owns more than 500 newspapers in hungary, which he edits at will. as i mentioned, orbán and his cronies are already expanding into other countries, such as slovenia and romania, where they use their newspapers to promote themselves and their friends. therefore, the european union, if it wants to protect its core values, one of which is freedom of the press, cannot open the money trap for orbán in order to protect hungarians and the population living abroad targeted by orbán.
in writing. although much progress has been made in europe in the field of freedom of expression in recent decades, this issue still remains a challenge even in today's democracies. over the years, techniques for restricting or circumventing the right to freedom of expression have been refined through strategic lawsuits to discourage public participation, known as slapp lawsuits. in addition, the commission has also been involved in the implementation of the un guidelines on the protection of the rights of persons with disabilities, which are based on the un guidelines on the protection of the rights of persons with disabilities. to date, no legislation exists in any member state against such litigation. this is problematic. the murder of maltese investigative journalist daphne caruana galizia, who was facing more than 40 civil and criminal cases when she was murdered in october 2017, was a wake-up call for europe. the european parliament therefore urges the european commission to establish an ambitious european legislative framework to protect media freedom and to use european funds to provide support to victims of slapp cases.
in writing. democracy, the rule of law and freedom of expression are among the eu's fundamental values. these are things that allow, among other things, the existence of a free media that acts as a watchdog of power and the investigation and exposure of possible abuses by those in power. it is therefore regrettable that in europe there is a widespread tendency to use legal action or the threat of legal action to silence those who are exercising their civil rights. such actions weaken the openness of society and silence the very voices we should be listening to more closely. it is particularly deplorable that in some cases this silence has been practiced even by the authorities of the member states. investigative journalists and ngos must be able to do their work in peace from legal harassment. it is excellent that the problem has been addressed, but it is not enough. we need action to ensure that the courts act as guardians of fundamental rights and the eu's fundamental values, not as their limiters. it remains to be seen whether the commission's proposal, which will be published at the beginning of the year, is sufficiently ambitious in this respect.
recommendation for second reading by mr regner and mr garcía del blanco on the disclosure of income tax information by certain companies and branches (2016/0107 ((cod)) a9-0305/2021 i would remind you that you can request the speeches and questions for the blue card through your voting machine after you have put your voting card in the machine. so i ask you to take your voting card with you at all times. instructions are available in the conference room. if you wish to request a request for an allowance, you can do so now and not at the end of the debate. i would also remind you that the floor in the chamber is still used for speeches from the chair in the centre of the chamber, with the exception of speeches on request, blue card questions and speeches on the agenda. i urge you to follow the list of speakers and move closer to the podium as your time to speak begins.
the rapporteur. the commission has already made a number of proposals. this was a team effort. i'll say this up front. a big thank you to all those who worked so hard on this - the shadow rapporteurs in particular, the finnish presidency, which was very committed here from 2019 onwards, but above all the portuguese presidency, which then brought this to a close with a lot of energy. i would also like to thank the rapporteur, mr garcía del blanco, who is also a member of the juri committee. so persistence pays off. i am proud to say today that the european parliament has not only heard the calls of the people for tax transparency for large corporations - yes, we have answered those calls, and our answer is public country-by-country reporting, public cbcr. this is the directive on country-specific tax transparency for corporations. this eu directive will for the first time require multinational companies to make their tax information public. for too long, big corporations have played by their own rules, and for too long we have had to watch as legislators. it's over now. and when we talk about tax justice, one thing is clear: if you want tax justice, you need tax transparency. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the tax system is not distorted. in 2016, five years ago, the panama papers shook the world. the panama papers uncovered countless cases of tax evasion and illegal activity around the world. and so they started the necessary debate on tax morality. our aim in this house, in the ep, is and has been to shine a light on the shadow world of taxation through public country-specific reporting. what have we achieved? we get the disclosure of tax and financial information: large multinational companies in the eu must disclose where they make profits, where they pay taxes and this also applies to countries in the eu tax havens. the target group is all companies with an annual turnover of more than eur 750 million operating in the eu. the public country reporting directive will enable us in the european union to put our own house in order - that is, we will finally have access to new information on all member states and their tax havens. and that's why this is so important, because about 80 percent of the profits that are shifted out of the european union are shifted into the eu tax havens, so mainly to ireland, luxembourg, the netherlands and others, as studies by the renowned economist gabriel zucman and others have shown. and that is where our policy comes in. we closed a very important transparency loophole. we have succeeded in transforming the comply-or-explain provision proposed by the council into a comply-and-explain provision. we are going after the letterbox companies: companies have to report how many full-time employees are employed not only in the parent company, but also in each subsidiary. we also strengthen trade unions in their collective bargaining. and most importantly, we get free and open access to corporate tax and financial data. it means that the company information is available to all of us, free of charge, in an open format and a common template on the corporate websites, among other things. all this and more will be implemented in the member states as early as 2023. thanks to these new rules, we will now know which companies are the front runners and which are making their fair share. the european union is leading the way here, and i am quite sure that many other countries will follow.
member of the commission. – madam president, first of all, let me thank the rapporteurs, evelyn regner and mr garcía del blanco, for their work in striking an interinstitutional agreement on the commission’s proposal for the disclosure of income tax information. i would also like to thank the shadows for their input. i would also like to express my appreciation for the hard work of the successive council presidencies over the last five years, especially ambassador pedro lourtie of the portuguese presidency, for enabling a positive conclusion on this file. a healthy single market needs a fair, efficient and growth-friendly tax system. we also need informed democratic debates and control to ensure public trust in our tax system. the commission made this proposal in 2015 in order to address the european citizens’ demand for transparency over large multinational enterprises by enabling public scrutiny on where their profits are won and where they pay corporate tax. this public country-by-country reporting will rebuild trust in our businesses and in the fairness of our tax systems. the text debated today requires all multinational enterprises with a turnover exceeding eur 750 million to publish the profits they make and the tax they pay in each member state of the union, as well as in non-cooperative jurisdictions for tax purposes. to complete the picture, enterprises will also disclose the aggregated figure on corporate tax paid outside the union. this country-by-country reporting will complement similar reporting already in force in the union for all banks, as well as for large companies in the extractive industry. in this respect, the european union is undoubtedly a frontrunner on such transparency for the greater public interest. let me conclude by saying that it is a pleasure to see the file debated today. this will hopefully be followed by a positive vote tomorrow. i am looking forward to hearing the debate, but one thing i know for sure is that our citizens will be observing what the union can do for them and how it is working to achieve an economy that works for the people.
i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. i would like to say that we are prepared as a group to support this proposal in the form of a resolution by the two committees responsible for examining the text. let me say that i personally welcome the fact that after all these years we have finally managed to get this material moving and that this is another concrete expression of how we are fighting tax evasion, profit diversion and what we have recently spoken about at our plenary meeting in strasbourg, where we commented on the international scandal and the so-called pandora papers case. the measure we are discussing today and approving tomorrow will also help to reduce the circle of those who do not want to pay their taxes honestly, who speculate and invent ways of diverting their profits, ways of preventing their profits from being taxed fairly. one of the defenses against this is the disclosure of information, and that's what this is about. i would like to thank both of my colleagues for their work, and the commissioner for her report. if there's enough publicity, it's going to be very difficult to divert profits, to hide profits, and to tax them in tax havens. i believe this proposal is in the public interest. the citizens of the european union should know what profits and what taxes are paid on those profits by large multinational companies operating in the european union. in addition, other information that is important for collective bargaining, for example, will be published. i'm convinced that this amount of information will do no harm to a large multinational corporation. the principle of proportionality is thus respected, sufficient privacy is maintained and information which is necessary to disclose in the public interest is disclosed. so we support the material.
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, after five years of deadlock in the council, we finally managed to seal a deal on the public country-by-country reporting, which is a crucial transparency tool. thanks to the work of this parliament, big multinationals will be obliged to publicly disclose where they make profits and where they pay their taxes. the agreement also includes the obligation for companies to publish how many full-time employees they have, their turnover and taxes paid, as well as all the profits and losses they have in each country they operate in – in the european union and in tax havens. thanks to this transparency, citizens will know which companies are freeriding and which are contributing their fair share to society. this is part of a bigger effort that we are leading towards creating a more just and fair society. along with the international tax agreement, a new wave of tax justice is now on the agenda of the european debate and of the international one, and we will keep pushing and step-by-step we will build a better union.
mr president, commissioner, rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, the european education area must be established as a strategic instrument to promote inclusiveness and accessibility for all. it must also be an opportunity to give vocational training a clear identity, like all other training paths. in recent days i have been confronted with a training centre of excellence in apulia, an example of good practice, able to make a concrete contribution to the construction of the area, first of all by making the mobility of students and teachers a reality; secondly, by enhancing the teaching profession in terms of initial education and professional development; the aim of the project is to develop a european network of experts in the field of digital skills and to develop a european network of experts in the field of digital skills. these, in my view, are some of the most effective interventions in creating a european education area that is truly accessible and inclusive.
mr president, i would like to thank you for your time. as shadow rapporteur for the empl opinion, i appreciate that the report reflects our priorities for education while maintaining the principle of subsidiarity. i also thank my colleague mrs šojdra for this balance. education should not be aimed exclusively at employment. education should primarily serve the personal development of the individual and be tailored to his individual needs. whether it is in relation to inclusion for people with disabilities, re-skilling when losing a job or wanting to develop further in old age. this is why, in addition to focusing on professional specialisations, we should also focus on so-called cross-cutting skills, such as media literacy, critical thinking, intercultural and interreligious competences. i see this concept of the european education area as an opportunity to break with the conventional view of education. let's open up the possibility of development for everyone, regardless of age, special needs or socioeconomic status, because only then can we create a society that is resilient to change and provides equal opportunities.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we know that there will be no recovery without a real commitment to education and training. today more than ever we need a european education area and an approach to education that is holistic, inclusive, integrated, that puts learners and the most vulnerable at the centre, that guarantees equal opportunities for all in accessing the labour market. concrete measures are needed, such as the automatic recognition of qualifications, periods of study abroad or volunteering, the development of european micro-credentials allowing the recognition of vocational training, as well as the recognition of the skills of third-country nationals - an important issue - to facilitate their access to education and employment. all of this has to come from an unprecedented financial commitment by member states, who we ask to allocate a good part of the recovery and resilience plan and public spending to education, because this is the real investment that looks to the future.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, since 2017 the european commission has presented various initiatives aimed at creating a european education area by 2025. the objectives to be achieved include the green and digital transition, increased collaboration between schools, the strengthening of foreign language learning and the adoption of the european student charter. these are ambitious goals, but they can be achieved if all students have equal access to education: i am referring in particular to students from the most vulnerable groups, to students with disabilities or learning difficulties. social inclusion must be the main challenge to be met in order to achieve a genuine european education area. the european union can and must ensure inclusive and accessible education for all european students. education is a right, no one should be left behind.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the cap is one of the points which gives meaning to the very existence of europe. we are not satisfied with too many downward compromises, we would have liked an immediate and more marked change. but to keep the old cap would have been suicide for european farmers and consumers. there are important steps forward. stop the money from exploiting workers, and that's a great achievement. the concept of animal welfare is being introduced and we will fight to ensure that it is respected more and more and against all cruelty. but we must do more for hemp, the plant of the future, and we must above all rely on agriculture as a pillar of the ecological transition and therefore of our future. the 60% of resources for green architecture, the 25% for ecosystems, which we would have liked to make compulsory and will have to make compulsory, and the 4% of inactive hectares are for us only the beginning of a green revolution, which must be carried out together with farmers, especially small farmers, and not against them. national plans and future trade agreements with other economies must ensure that strict ecological standards are met. never again will we import products that do not meet the standards we impose on our producers. there is a rich green future for our europe and it is up to us to make it happen.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the agreement reached in the trilogue is crucial because it guarantees a new cap integrated, for the first time, by a green architecture and social conditionality, but i think the real challenge begins tomorrow. member states will have to be able to incorporate the new principles into their national strategic plans, supporting thousands of small farmers, especially young people and women, to innovate and make their production sustainable by guaranteeing their fair income. the alignment of the cap with the green new deal will therefore need to combine legal certainty with ambitious, realistic targets and monitoring. social conditionality will have to defeat the corporate and reward those who create quality employment, allowing the deduction of labour costs in the calculation of the capping. it is therefore time to extend the country of origin requirement to all products, to reject misleading labelling schemes such as nutri-score, to ensure a strong external projection of the cap, and to ensure that environmental, health and social standards are reciprocated in trade agreements. only by combining the local and global dimensions, as well as the three pillars of sustainability, can we truly strengthen the agri-food sector, making it the vanguard of the green and social revolution that the new europe intends to represent.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the glasgow conference has produced, let us say, frankly disappointing results. let me be clear: the protection of the environment is a top priority, but the ecological challenge must be faced with pragmatism and seriousness, not with ideology. the green transition, as envisioned by the european commission, has costs that are often not considered. the european race towards climate neutrality, unaccompanied by the race of giants such as russia, china, india and partly also the united states, risks being a burden only for our continent. europe accounts for only 7% of global emissions. the european taxpayers, and in particular the poorer classes, are likely to be the only ones to pay the price for the wrong choices, as we have already seen in france with the high price of petrol. at the same time, the dramatic rise in energy prices is partly linked to certain green decisions. these meetings, where hundreds of leaders arrive by plane, are objectively in danger of being a showcase of hypocrisy and broken promises. let us not forget that china is already the leader in many of the commodity products of the future. i'm thinking about lithium in electric vehicle batteries, the semimetals that are critical for wind turbines and even solar panels. in short, our production risks being penalized by unsustainable constraints and becoming totally dependent on the chinese giant. the environment must be preserved at all costs, but we must have the foresight to calculate the social and economic consequences of the choices we make. the risk is that we will see further relocations and further redundancies.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the question today should not be how to vaccinate the world, but why we should do it. we all call experimental mrna or dna gene therapies that don't immunise "vaccines", because those who receive them can get covid-19 and spread it, and they only partially reduce the likelihood of severe disease for a few months. on the other hand, thousands of adverse reactions, including very serious and disabling ones, and continuous deaths after inoculation are recorded every day as a result of administration, the causal correlation of which with the injected products has already been established in many cases. the precautionary principle is therefore being neglected. we also allow for very serious discrimination, deprivation and violence against those who do not accept inoculation for medical, immunological or personal reasons, in violation of fundamental human rights. now do we want to commit more money from european citizens to extend this treatment to the whole world? who are you? in the name of god, colleagues, let's stop this madness!

in writing. the guidelines presented by commissioner dalli in the 'union of equality' document represent yet another lack of respect for millions of european citizens. in the name of inclusiveness, the european commission even deletes the term "christmas", urging european citizens to adopt a language that is "non-discriminatory". but the fact that we are trying to include it is likely to have the opposite effect. christendom, or europe, is not just the title of novalis's 1799 essay, but a phrase that perfectly sums up our origins. the european union is rooted in judeo-christian traditions and values, and its flag is inspired by the icon of the virgin mary. to deny all this would be to deny your own identity. in my view, a government or institution which imposes its own way of speaking on its citizens cannot at the same time call itself democratic. finally, i believe that issues such as this risk greatly diminishing the very low level of public confidence in the european union, especially in the present context, with far more important challenges to be faced.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, commissioner, honourable members, the recovery and resilience facility is at the heart of the eu response to the coronavirus virus crisis. it will play a central role in mitigating the economic and social impact of the pandemic and delivering a more sustainable and resilient european union, better prepared for the challenges and opportunities of the green and digital transition. beyond its economic impact, the facility is a strong sign of european unity and ambition. the unprecedented solidarity shown through the rrf requires everyone to use its funds responsibly and efficiently. the facility also offers a unique opportunity to enhance investment coordination across the eu, and joint coordinated action at european level is more effective and benefits member states more than uncoordinated individual national efforts, not least due to a significant spillover effect across countries. the rrf, embedded in the european semester of economic and employment policy coordination, will also become a key tool to implement the policy reform agenda in the years ahead. the reforms included in the national recovery and resilience plan will be instrumental in effectively addressing the country-specific recommendations identified in the context of the european semester. hence, national parliaments will play a key role in the timely adoption of the legislative reforms to allow the full implementation of the plans. a successful rrf requires not only the effective use of its funds, but also a sound and credible financial structure. i very much welcome the fact that the commission has already raised over eur 70 billion of next generation eu funds, and that it has attracted great interest among international investors. we have come a long way since the adoption of the facility in february. all member states, but one, have submitted their rrps. the commission has assessed 22 plans and the council has acted swiftly on the related commission proposals and adopted all 22 plans, with the tight timeline set out in the regulation. the rrf has featured on the agendas of all the ecofin councils during this presidency and three extraordinary informal council meetings. they were also there to accelerate the adoption of the process. the council stands ready to examine, without delay, the proposals that the commission will make for the pending rrps and to ensure that they all meet the requirements for a positive assessment. overall, the plans approved by the council so far represent eur 291 billion in non-repayable financing and eur 154 billion in loans. member states have allocated almost 40% of spending in their plans to climate measures and more than 26% to the digital transition, exceeding the agreed targets of 37% and 20% respectively. the adoption of the council implementing decisions has allowed the disbursement since august 2021 of more than eur 50 billion in pre—financing to 18 member states, boosting recovery efforts across the union. with the approval of the recovery and resilience plans and the first funds distributed to member states, the focus now crucially turns to the implementation phase. throughout this phase, checking and monitoring will be vital if the rrf, as a whole, is to be a lasting success. member states have started to submit their payment requests for the implementation of their plans. each request will require verification that the relevant milestones and targets are properly met. we, as the co-legislators, have no formal role in the assessment of these requests, which are carried out by the commission in compliance with the regulation. for its part, the council will continue to monitor the implementation of the facility and, as appropriate, provide political impetus and guidance to unroll its full potential. as i said, the implementation of the recovery and resilience plans will be a key element of the economic surveillance and policy coordination in the context of the european semester. the council remains committed to continuing its close engagement with all interested parties at every step of the way to make the rrf a success.
executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, it is a pleasure to be back in this house. i would like to update you on where we stand on economic recovery and in particular on the recovery and resilience facility (rrf). we are well on track and looking forward to next year as a year of implementation. we are now emerging from the crisis and the recovery and resilience facility will help us to prepare also for the future. to date, the council has adopted implementing decisions for 22 national recovery and resilience plans. the financing is starting to flow. the commission is still assessing plans from four countries: bulgaria, hungary, poland and sweden. on bulgaria, we are making good progress. for sweden, the next steps will depend on the orientations that the new government will take. discussions continue with both hungary and poland. our aim is to arrive at a positive assessment in due course, once the conditions and criteria set out in the rrf regulation are met. the netherlands has not yet presented a plan. we are continuing to work constructively with the dutch authorities, which have indicated that they expect the plan to be submitted in spring 2022. on pre-financing: so far, we have provided eur 54.2 billion to 18 member states. the last disbursement was to romania and we expect to make disbursements to estonia and malta shortly. earlier today we launched the online scoreboard for tracking progress on the facility overall and each recovery and resilience plan, together with specific reporting on social expenditure. we are advancing with operational arrangements, which will detail further technical conditions for monitoring the implementation. last month, the commission signed arrangements with the first two member states: spain and france. both have submitted their first payment requests. for france, the payment request is under evaluation. for spain, the commission has approved its request for eur 10 billion in grants. before any disbursement, however, the economic and financial committee must give its opinion. we expect the next requests to be submitted early next year and will thoroughly assess each one in full respect of the rrf regulation. parliament will continue to receive all due information as required. i am confident that parliament will be an important ally throughout the implementation phase. we also understand that parliament will prepare its own review report on the rrf, which should feed into the commission’s review report due in july 2022. we very much welcome this step. thank you, and commissioner gentiloni will now have further points to add.
member of the commission. – madam president, as valdis dombrovskis has just said, we have 22 plans adopted by the council, and in these plans we see a well-balanced representation of the six pillars. the total estimated cost of the 22 plans amounts to eur 450 billion. let me give you a first survey of the measures put forward by member states in their plans, measures that, of course, can contribute to more than one of the pillars with their primary and secondary assignment, so the total adds up to more than 100%. the first results: 50% of measures will support the green transition; 29% will support the digital transition; 50% will support smart, sustainable and inclusive growth; 43% will support social and territorial cohesion; 17% health, economic and social resilience; and 11% of measures will support policies for the next generation, including education and skills. all this data will be available in the scoreboard that has been launched today. now, as we have received the first payment requests, i would like to assure you that the commission remains committed to being thorough and acting as quickly as possible. for spain, we will now await the economic and financial committee opinion and then proceed with the first disbursement. we are committed to making the process as transparent as possible. we have published the operational arrangements signed with spain and france, and we will encourage each member state to agree to this publication. we will also publish our preliminary assessment of the milestones and targets to ensure that the broader public can follow how the facility continues to deliver at eu level and in their own country. exactly for this reason, the recovery and resilience scoreboard has been launched today. not all data is available yet, so the information provided on the scoreboard will continue to grow in the coming months. for example, we are still waiting for the first input by member states on the agreed common indicators, which will be available in spring 2022. the support from the facility can reach the entire society in the union, and this is strongly needed now, when we are still fighting the pandemic but with the recovery underway. visibility will be crucial for the success. we will step up our efforts to communicate the role and the impact of next generation eu. together, the parliament and the commission, we can make the difference. together, we can fulfill this extraordinary mission for the next generation of europeans.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, commissioners, minister, welcome to the european parliament. almost exactly one year ago in december 2020, we managed to agree between the european parliament, the council and the commission on the largest package of economic support ever created by the european union: the recovery and resilience facility. much was achieved in this one year, since, with 22 plans already approved by the commission and the council, and with the countries having received their pre-financing, we are now moving from the assessment to the implementation phase. the scope is clear – recovery and resilience. we have to use the funds to help people, enterprises and regions affected by the virus in the process of recovery, but resilience is also important. we have to use the money to strengthen our public systems, healthcare, education, the economy, the public systems and the private sector, so that if ever in the future such a crisis hits us, we will not be hit in the same way. we are going to spend more money than ever. this is why control is important. the european public prosecutor, the european court of auditors and the european anti-fraud office need to have a strong role. we made sure that there are clear rules at european level, but that there is also flexibility. countries can use the money where they need it, but one thing cannot be negotiated, and that is values and that is the rule of law. if money flows, european values should be respected. furthermore, the money should also be used to reform; it should not be seen only as a source of investment. investments and reforms go hand in hand. i expect the commission to observe the implementation of all the country-specific recommendations, and some are very important. fiscal sustainability is essential. it is very important because we could only create this recovery and resilience facility now because our member states are still credible enough in the eyes of the financial markets so that the guarantee that we gave to the commission enabled the commission to access funds. we need to ensure fiscal sustainability again in the future. we, as parliament, are going to monitor the work of the commission and the implementation of this plan.
the commission has already taken the necessary steps to ensure that the recovery plan is implemented. as has been said here, most national plans have been submitted and most national programmes approved so far. prefinancing arrived in the summer and now it is time to sign the operating agreements and, above all, to present the first reports with the milestones and objectives achieved. at the moment, only spain has done so, but i really hope that the other countries will speed up and submit them as soon as possible, because that will mean that the whole preparation phase will have been completed and we will be fully involved in implementation. and for that, 2022 is going to be key. and it's also going to be key because we're going to have a lot of data to be able to make an assessment of the plan. in february they will also have to submit their annual report and, among other things, they will have to evaluate and quantify the expenditure that has been made for each of the six pillars and social expenditure, with a relative breakdown in the field of children and youth. you know that my group has been very critical of the selection of social and gender indicators, and we want it to be improved. we want to be rigorous, we need to have the best tools and, moreover, if we want to be rigorous - and this is my last sentence - we must respect the rule of law. without respect for the rule of law, there can be no financing of the recovery plan or the european budgets.
in the name of the id group. madam president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, table 2 on page 7 of the 'recovery and resilience dialogue with the european commission' forecasts the growth of gdp per country due to ngeu from 0.4/0.7% until 2026 for germany and austria and to 1.5/2.5% for italy until 2026 and for spain until 2024. this means that in the best case scenario, the growth generated is estimated at an average of 0.14% per annum for the former countries and 0.52% for the latter. on the basis of these considerations, it follows that the type of investments rigidly schematised in the ngeu agenda will produce a low multiplier, considerably lower than that produced by the respective deficit expenditure. the commission notes that the commission has not provided any information on the amount of the aid granted to ngeu in the period from 1 january 2014 to 31 december 2015. therefore, only after having estimated the multipliers of these consolidation measures could we be able to predict with more reasonable certainty whether and to what extent ngeu will lead to positive net growth, pending a rapid and constructive review of the entire economic governance of the union.
